CAPITAL" CHAI.

Everyone knows the story of the pressman who, when asked at one of General Booth's meetings if he were saved, replied that he was a reporter. The following, of which the scene is laid in Melbourne, is reported to have occurred during the General's visit to that city. One ot the reporters for an evening paper rose to leave the hall with his copy. "Brother," cried the General," you will be d-- if you go now." "I shall be d-- by the sub-editor if I don't go," was the reply. "But the Lord is greater than the sub-editor," said the General warn- ingly. "The sub-editor does not think so," was the retort.

It has been said lately that the Dowager Empress of Germany contemplates residing permanently in England. As far as the German Emperor is con- cerned the arrangement is said to be perfectly satis- factory, but the German Chancellor has yet to give his consent, which is unlikely, unless the Empress Frederick consents to give up part of her German allowances. From England the Empress Frederick receives an allowance of ten thousand pounds a year. But fronr Germany she receives, "all told," a larger allowance than comes into the coffers of Queen Victoria as reigning sovereign of England.

Appley Hall, Norfolk, is to be the residence of Princess Maud of Wales and Prince Charles of Den- mark after their marriage; and the county has de- cided to celebrate that fact by giving a county wedding present to the royal couple. A committee has been formed, at the suggestion of the Earl of Leicester, and subscriptions are to be limited to three guineas. Norwich, as the county town, will give a separate wedding present of its own, the subscription being limited to two guineas,the desire of the committee being to obtain small sums, and so make the present from the citizens generally.

Yet greater efforts are to be made by the Austra- lian colonies, backed by their respective Govern- ments, to capture the English food markets, to the further exclusion of home producers. The New South Wales Ministry, following the lead set by the neighbouring colony of Victoria, has determined to constitute a board for export. The department will conduct a thorough system of inspection, gradiog and branding, issuing certificates for all classes of exports. The Government will undertake, more- over, to provide cheap and reliable freight arrange- ments throughout the different seasons, and will station in London an officer qualified to report upon the condition of the various shipments on arrival. The timber growers will also endeavour to secure the grading and classification of all timbers for ex- port. The Government ot Australia are consider- ing a proposal that all meat exported shall bear a uniform brand, indicating that it has been passed by the Government inspectors as sound and whole- some.

Those English noblemen who go to the United States to wed heiresses are threatened with a tariff. A bill before the New York Legislature proposes to levy a tax upon the dowries of the women who marry foreign noblemen in that State, or whose dowry is paid out of the revenues ot property there. The tax is to be 2 per cent. of the total amount of the dot, and the revenues thus secured are to be devoted to the founding of manual train- ing schools for females and for the building and maintenence of homes for aged and destitute women.

It ;s probable that the Government bill for free- ing the highroads to horseless carriages will be brought in the House of Lords next week.

Mrs. Hirst Alexander, a well-known Australian journalist, now making a short visit in London, says that marked good effects have followed the enfranchisement of women in New Zealand. The Premier of New Zealand, the Hon. Richard J. Seddon, and the principal journals, have acknow- ledged, she says, that the influence of women dur- ing the elections and after has been beneficial. The women have turned their attention to ameliorating the conditions of prisons, promoting the welfare of children, and interesting themselves in other mea-, sures with which they are specially qualified to deal.

As soon as the announcement was made that the Government intended to send an expedition in the direction of Dongola, the suspicion became current 'in Radical quarters that it was Mr. Chamberlain who had forced this policy upon Lord Salisbury. Mr. Labouchere's insinuations to this effect on Monday were rather an echo of outside speculation than .an original effort of that ingenious cynic's imagination. Sir W. Harcourt had evidently formed. the same view as the critics of the Radical clubs, Judging from the pains he took to emphasize Mr.. Chamberlain's old and strongly expressed hos- tility to. an advance into the Soudan, and also to ostentatiously claim Mr. Chamberlain's co-operation in condemning any such policy. Mr. Labouchere likewise made a strong point of Lord Salisbury's formerly avowed personal opposition to any at tempt to reconquer the Soudan: and as the was nobody in the Cabinet worth singling out for at- tack except Mr. Charaherlain,the Colonial Secretary inferences that came to be enmeshed in a web of that he is the real author of the present adventure. But the truth is that we have not yet got. at the bottom of this new Egyptian policy, which goes much deeper than any mere passing opinion of Mr. Chamberlain's. It will be found that the elements which have led to it are most simple but complex; and that while Soudanese considerations are chiefly operative at Cairo, European ones are chiefly opera- tive at Sr. James's.

What are these European considerations? Are they a desire to render help to Italy and to produce a diversion on its behalf? Nobody believes it, for ceortraphy and an impassable desert are against it. Is there some secret arrangement with Germany, whereby England gives her moral support to the Triple Alliance, and thereby detaches herself from France and Russia in exchange for Germany's sur- render of its designs upon the Transvaal? Germany is thus supposed to gain by the re-establishment of unfriendly relations between England and France: and England is supposed to gain by the buying off of German hostility in the Transvaal, and by Ger- man sanction of our hold upon Egypt, Whatever the explanation may he of this Nile campaign, the Dervishes are not the cause of it. We must seek for it in the tortuous courses of European diplo- macy, and the secret will not be easily wrung from the Government. It will find its way to the public by some backstairs channel in due time; but every- thing points to a new European combination, or perhaps rather to a restoration of an old one. In- genious speculations are everywhere abundant; but I scarcely meet a man who believes that the ad- vance is dictated by any Egyptian emergency.

Wednesday night's meeting of the British O. al. i- thologicaliClub was made notable by the exhibition of no less than 900 "clutches" of the eggs of the common cuckoo with the eggs of the foster Parents. This we are not greatly surprised to hear is the largest number ever brought together on any occasion, and it testifies eloquently to the procrea- tive industry of that casual bird, as well as to its reprehensible lack of true parental instinct. Mr. Edward Bidwell was the proud possessor and exhi- bitor of this"finely assorted lot ."Another interesting feature of the gathering was the exhibition of Mr. Edward }Tnrgitt's lifa-like paintings of woodpeckers upon which that veteran ornithologist Dr. Bowdler Sharpe had much of interest to say.

There are rumours of considerable dissatisfaction with some of the provisions understood to have been inserted in the draft of the Government Edu- cation Bill. The difficulties encountered owing to conflicting views are understood to be chiefly re- sponsible for the delay in bringing the measure be- fore Parliament.

President Kruger's visit to England is now regarded at certain as Pretoria. The President says it is true that a section of the burghers is opposed to his coming to this country, but he is convinced that the opposition will disappear when the negotiations on the points to be discussed are arranged.

All rights reserved. THINGS A WOMAN WANTS TO KNOW.

THE numerous varieties of soft silk and crepe, which are to be found on all sides, and in the most enchanting shades, tempt us to indulge in one or more pretty blouses of this material. No blouse looks dressy unless there be a great deal of material expended on it, and then it should be made in a picturesque, quaint form.

The sketch which I am giving you below is of a blouse recently brought over from Paris

A French Blouse. No. 1912. Paper Pattern, CA To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA STREET, LONDON, W.C.

by a friend of mine. It is made of a delicate shade of pale green silk, covered with'a blurred design of flowers.

Tlie zouave portion, which gives such t delightful finish to the whole, was of rich green velvet, bordered with iridescent passementerie. The fitted waistband, which you will notice is perfectly plain, was made of a rich oorded silk, and ornamented with two cut steel buttons on either side of the front. The dainty silk blouse you see is made very full. It has a high stand-up collar with a ruching of lace coming from the inner side, and projecting in the form of a ruffle. The lace thus used is cascaded down the front, giving the appearance of a jabot. The sleeves look particularly pretty, for though very full they are by no means exaggerated, the silk being " rucked " from elbow to wrist.

If-you want to be exceedingly smart during the early spring, let me advise you to have a blouse made as soon as possible from this model. Wear it with an alpaca or silk skirt, and you will have the most chic costume you can desire. Petticoats for day wear have almost gone out >f use. I have been told at some of the lead- g houses for ladies' underwear that there is hardly any demand for flannel petticoats, ladies who go in for active amusements finding tweed knickerbockers much more convenient and far more comfortable.

Though we gladly wear these garments made of tweed throughout the winter, in summer we find the material somewhat warm. Let me, therefore, recommend you to make knicker- bockers of satin or alpaca; the latter is, of

Ladies' Alpaca Knickerbockers. No. 1943. Paper Pattern, sp. To be had from PATTERN DEPARTMENT, 18, HENRIETTA STREET, LONDON, W.C.

course, the less expensive. The dress skirt slips beautifully over it, and if made in drab or silver grey it washes extremely well. The design here given buttons on either side and is fitted with darts in the front. At the back it is made with a sloped band, the material being gathered into its lower edge. Flannel, I am perfectly certain, should be worn by all, young and old alike, by day and night. Many people object to flannel night- dresses on the score of their shrinking. I have lately discovered that Ceylon flannel has not this aggravating quality, in fact nightdresses made of it wear exceedingly well, and if prettily trimmed with Torchon lace and finished with silk feather-stitching, a really charming gar- ment is the result. Bear this in mind, you mothers of little ones, and use this material for your girls and boys, making of it either night- dresses or sleeping suits, according to your fancy. The spring is apt to be a terribly trying timg for people with sensitive skins, and I knoNg many ladies who suffer much at this season with chapped hands. I would advise them to use k simple and most efficacious remedy which I have never known to fail, _

Take equal parts of the best sweet oil and glycerine and mix them, afterwards adding two or three drops of essential oil of lavender. Apply a little of this to the hands every night on going to bed and " chaps " and sore hands will be unknown.

At this time of the year all good housewives indulge in that terrible process, is clean- ing. I call it terrible, for that s the term by which the process is designated in the house- hold. All the same, it is one that must be endured. Let me remind you before relaying your carpets to see that the floors are scrubbed with some disinfectant soap, and leave them to dry thoroughly for a whole day. Before putting down the carpets, cover the floor carefully with brown paper, for this is a great saving to the carpet. There is a brown paper sold specially for this purpose in largo sheets, but as this is an expense some of us cannot afford to incur, let me advise you to keep all wrapping-paper, and use it for the purpose.• When the floor is covered, sprinkle pepper well over it before laying the carpet, as a preventitive of moth. The present system of having squares of carpet rather than one which fits closely to the room, is very economical, for in this way it can be turned every time it is taken up, and the ?roar more evenly distributed.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY MARCH 21. 1896 THE HIDDEN HAND; OR,

Perfumes were introduced into Europe by the Arabs. who brought many receipts for making them from the East.

More than seven hundred million needles are said to be made each w ek within the limits of the town of Red- ditch.

There are two or three old ladies in a large Continen- tal city who make it their business to feed stray cats. One feeds more than one hundred daily. The temperature of the cucumber is one degree below that of the surrounding atmosphere. It is,therefore, ap- parent that the expression "cool as a cucumber" is scientifically correct. The most trustworthy signs of freshness in fish of every kind are the brightness and prominence of the eyes. And the redness and ricridity of the gills. Fish which is unseasonable will display a want of solidity and firmness,with a bluish-pearly appearance where it should be white.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

There will be a great run on the jewel markets this year, for three Royal weddings and a coronation need considerable furnishing in the matter of precious stones. Moreover the Kaiser is negotiating for the purchase of some magnificent diamonds, with the intention of hav- ing them set in the Imperial Crown. The German Im- perial family possess many very valuable diamonds. Among them is tne " Regent" one of the most regular and valuable diamonds extant. For a time it decorated the hilt of the sword of Napoleon 1., but the sword fell into the hands of the Germans after the Battle of Water- loo, and has ever since belonged to the Kings of Prussia. Queen Victoria's " Kohinoor " is largeL than the " Regent." though not so lovely a stone. *.,Wit k regard to single pearls, the Queen has in her possession one of the largest in the world, but the one which eclipses all others is supposed to be that known as "La Pellgrina " in the Museum of Zosima, in Moscow. The qualities which in jewellers' language, a fine pearl possessed, are a perfect" skin "and fine " orient " which means a delicate texture free from speck or flaw, and of clear, almost translucent sheen.

There appears to be more and more difficulty each year in obtaining a good likeness if her Majesty. In the first place the photographer who has been" com- manded " never knows up to the last moment when or where her Majesty will consent to receive him, and be taken—and. again, it is by no means easy to make bar look as tall as is desirable, for the Queen is proverbially impatient of the camera and absolutely refuses to pose longer than a few seconds. The Princess of Wales on the other hand is one of the most gracious and interested of sitters. She knows immediately haw to pose herself and not infrequently suggests improvements in her own handwriting on the back of the proofs which have been submitted to her. Apropos, the German Empress is an adept with the camera,and often when staying with her husband at one of his shooting boxes, she will accom- pany him on his expeditions, and take photographs of any quaint or beautiful scene which she may happen to pass. Miss Ellen Terry's pet hobby is photographs. When travelling she always has with her a large case of family pictures. This is of leather and folds up. She is exceedingly fond of being photographed, though she often says that she does not care to expose herself to the deadly camera now so freely as when she was younger._

Tdere is no doubt that the majority of us have always been under the impression that protession Al musicians let their hair grow long as an advertisement: or a pro- test against the close-cropped head of the Philistine, but this according to a scientist is not so. The learned man attributes the plentiful crops to the music and main- tains that the music of certain instruments is a very powerful hair tonic. In support of his theory he cites the portraits of Paderewski, Vladimir de Pachman, Joseph Hofman, Johannes Wolf, Burmeister. N-ichez, Barasate, and also Frida Scotta,the violinist. Speaking of Paderewski reminds me that he seems to be making a great success of his tour in the United States. I noticed the other day that an American paper, with characteris- tic exaggeration described him as " a musician with lemon-coloured hair, who lulls us into a hypnotic trance !" - -

One sad result of the change in sleeves has already come to our etas. It seems that an enterprising German '• middleman "quite recently purchased from a too in- ventive Yankee some tons of a new paper invention for distending sleeves, and no sooner had the purchase been effected than Dame Fashion decreed that sleeves must no longer distend but collapse altogether. There is very little doubt that the inventive Yankee knew all about this,and the enterprising Tenton didn't,anyway the con- sequence is that the speculative purchaser has been forted to store his too extensive supply of dry goods in a repository in the city, until the happy day (for him) when large sleeves, or anything else that wants stiffen- ing out, comes in again. The " Robespierre " stock-like collar and jabot is to be much worn, so soon as we find it possible to dispense with the cosy far necklets we have indulged in during the winter. It may be carried out in various ways, but is most correct in white satin, covered with folded net and completed by that full jabot of lace in front which was worn with so much effect by men of the period indicated. Writing of that time reminds ma,that sundry gew-gaws of that day, notably the black velvet pelerine, studded with steel brads of very minute siza, with scallopped edge and fringed ends, whose secret purpose I cannot determine, have reappeared. The pelerine burst upon my astonished gaze in Bond-street the other day,and I am now hourly expecting China crepe shawls, reticules and such like things.

Many of the newest silk petticoats are really exquisite. They are being shown not only in pale or dark coloured silks, but in beautiful brocades, and shot silks and satins, and are covered with a network of Persian leaves and figures of various patterns. Five yards round the hem is the correct width with a full flounce and frayed- out ruche matching the chief colour of the fabric. _ .

_ I spent a delightful hour or two one day last week at a smart establishment devoted entirely to millinery and noted among cther things a number of aigrettes made of wheat-ears, instead of the plumes of the unfortunate heron. This is indeed a move in the right direction, but oh ! those wheat-ears ! What would farmers say to them ? Some are blue, some are a brilliant orange and others are a bright red. Confronted with a waving field of such, the unfortunate farmer would indeed deem himself demented. But luckily, nature follows fashion no more than fashion follows nature. Geraniums and roses are to be shot in several colours this year as roses were last. A green and mauve pelargonium, a blue and purple rose, and, a blue and scarlet wallflower, biing among the novelties of the season.

Oxford appears to be quite hopeless with regard to the Women's Degree Question. Being beaten on the degree, the women asked for a diploma, being refused that,thev asked for a certificate. The answer is that they shall have neither degree, diploma, certificate, nor anything. The University will commit itself to no such perilous first step as is implied in admitting the existence of women students. The University must not be "mixed.' There seems at present nothing else for aspiring stedente to do but possess themselves in patience, and remember that there was a time when the University behaved in much the same way to those members of the opposite sex who were not members of the Church of England. Ira that case it had to accept the referne from without. Mdlle. Rosa Bonheur celebrated her 74th birthday on the 16th inst, the same date being fixed for the private view in London of the veteran artist's latest picture "The Duel." The work exhibits all the old power of the artist both in the firm vigourous handline, the sure and truthful drawing and the fine sense of colour The duel is one between horses, the famous Godolphin Arabian .and Flobgohlin,the one a splendid black stallion, the other nearly white. Mdlle. Rosa Bnnheur lives in a remote little village n the South cf France, on the out- skirts of an extensive forest. In her greunds,which she seldom quits, the famous artist has a large menaeerie, containing the models of most of her famous pictures. She has studied these animals from day to day, person- ally attending to their wants. In the 'course of time a watm affection hag grown up between the painter and " model " of which the mos remarkable proof was given when a lioness died almost in her arms.

My readers should remembc r that turpentine is the hest friend housekeepers have, and a supply :ghoul() al- ways be kept op hand. It is goc d for burns, excellent for corns, good for rheumatism and sore throat, and a quick remedy for fits or convulsions. It is a sure pre- ventive against moths, a few drops rendering garments safe from such invasion during the summer. For clean- ing paint, add a spoonful to a pail of warm water. A little in the suds on a washing day makes washing • easier.

Victoria Sandwich—Take the weight of three ergs in flour, butter, and castor sugar. Line a shallow, flat tin with a piece of well-greased paper. Put the butter and rugar info a basin, and beat them with a wooden spoln,till quite soft like cream. Take one tabl,spoonful ef flour and mix with it one teaspoonful of baking powder and place on one side. Now add one egg to the sugar and butter, shake over it one tablespoonful of fleur and mix well in. Go on adding eggs and flour till both are nqed up. Mix well, then add the flour that had the bak- ing powder mixed with it a few drops of vanilla and one tablespoonful of milk. Pour the mixture into a tin and bake for about ten minutes. Turn out, set it wide to cool, then spread one half with jam, lay the other half on top, cut into fingers and serve.

CAPITOLA THE MADCAP. BY MRS. E. D. E. N. SOUTHWORTH.

CHAPTER XXVIII.—(CO2Vintted). It was arranged that Father Gray, accompanied by Mrs. Condiment, should go to the gaol upon the following morning ; and accordingly they set out immediately after breakfast. A short ride up the mountain brought them to Tip Top, in the centre of which stood the gaol. It was a simple structure of grey stone, containing within its own walls the apartments occupied by the warden. To these, Mrs. Condiment, who was the leader in the whole matter, first presented herself, introducing Father Gray as one of the preachers of the camp-meeting, a very pious man, and very effective in his manner af dealing with hardened offenders. "I have heard of the Reverend Mr. Gray, and his powerful exhortations," said the warden, with a low bow; "and I hope he may be able to make some impression on these obdurate men, and induce them, if possible, to make a clean breast of it, and give up the retreat of their band. Each of them has been offered a free pardon on condition of turning State's evidence, and each has refused." " Indeed ; have they done so, case-hardened creatures ?" midly inquired Father Gray. "Aye, have they! but you, dear sir, may be able to persuade them to do so."

(‘ I shall endeavour—l shall endeavour," said the mild old man."

The warden then requested the visitors to follow him, and led the way up stairs to the cells. "I understand that tne criminals are confined separately ?" said Mr. Gray to the warden. " No, sir; they were so confined at first, for better security; but as they have been very quiet, and as since those rowdies that disturbed the ramp-meeting have been sent to prison, and filled up our cells, we have had to put those three robbers into one cell !"

"Pm afraid, I—" began the minister, hesi- tating. "Father Gray is nervous, good Mr. Gaoler; I hope there's no danger fr.un those dreadful men— all of them together—for I promised Father Gray that he should be safe, myself," said Mrs. Condi- ment.

"Oh, ma'am, undoubtedly; they are double- Ironed," said the warden, as he unlocked a door and admitted the visitors into rather a darkish cell, in which were the three prisoners. Steve, the mulatto, was stretched upon the floor in a deep sleep. Hal was sitting on the side of the cot, twiddling his fingers.

Dicic' sat crouched up in a corner, with his head against the wall.

Peace be with you, my poor souls," said the mild old man, as he entered the cell. "You go to the demon 1" said Dick, with a hideous scowl.

"Nay, my poor man. I came in the hope of savin," you from that enemy of souls." "Here's another : There's three comes reglar ! here's the fourth ! Go it, old fellow ! We're gettin' used to it ! gettin' to be entertainin' ! It's the only diversion we have in this blamed hole!" said Hal.

"Nay, friend, if you use profane language, I cannot stay to hear it, 'sail the old man. " Yaw-ww-aw-ow !" yawned Steve, half rising and stretching himself. 1. What's the row was just dreaming our captain had come to deliver us —yaw- am aw-o,h I it's only another parson ? " and With that, Steve turned himself over and settled to sleep. "My dear Mr. Gaoler—do you think that these men are safe ?—for if you do, I think we had better leave excellent Mr. Gray to talk to them alone— he can do them so much good, if ha has them idl to himself," said Mrs. Condiment, who was in spite of all her previous boasting, beginning to quail and tremble under the hideous glare of Demon Dick's eyes. " N-no ! n-no ! n-no 1" faltered the preacher, nervously taking hold of the coat of the warden. "You go alow_t out of this! the whole on you. I'm not a wild beast in a cage to be stared at ! " growled Demon Dick, with a baleful glare that sent Mrs. Condiment and the preacher to the cell door.

"Mr. Gray, I do assure you, sir, there is no dam. er ! the men are double-ironed, and malignant as they may be, they can do you no harm. And if you uxuld stay a.nd talk to them you might per- suade them to confession and do the community much service," said the warden. " I—l—l'm no coward! But but but—" faltered the old man, tremblingly approaching the prisoners:

"I understand you, sir. You are in bad health, which makes you nervous." "Yes, yes, Heaven forgive me; but if you, Mr. Gaoler, and this good lady here, will keep within call, in case of aceidents, I don't mind if I do re- main and exhort these men, for a short time," said the old man. "Of course we will. Come, Mrs. Condiment, mum! there's a good bench in the lobby, and I'll send for my old woman, and we three can have a good talk while the worthy Mr. Gray is speaking to the prisoners," said the warden, conducting the housekeeper from the cell. As soon as they had gone, the old man went to the door and peeped after them, and having seen that they went to the extremity of the lobby to a seat under an open window, he turned back to the cell, and going up to Hal, said in a low voice: "Now, then, is it possible that you do not know me? " Hal stopped twiddling his fingers and looked up at the tall, thin, stooping figure, the grey hair, the white eyebrows and the pale face, and said gruffly: _

"No! May the demon fly away with me if I ever I saw you before !" "Nor you, Dick ?" inq-ired the old man, in a mild voice, turning to the one addressed. "No, burn you nor want to see you now !" "Steve ! Steve !" said the old man, in a pitiful voice, waking the sleeper. "Don't you know me, either ?" "Don't bother me," said that worthy, giving himself another turn and another settle to sleep. " Dolts ! blockheads! brutes! do you know me now," growled the visitor, changing his voice. "Out Captain!" -

" Our Captain i"

"Our Captain !" they simultaneously cried. " llusie, sink your souls ! Do you want to bring the warden upon us ?" growled Black Donald, for it was unquestionably him in a new metamor- phosis. "Then all I have to say, Captain, is that you have left us here a blamed long time !" "And exposed you to sore temptation to peach on me! Couldn't help it, lads ! couldn't help it! I waited until I could do something to the pur- pose." "Now, may Satan roast me alive if I know what you have done to turn yourself into an old man! Burn my soul! if I should know you now, Captain, if it wa'n't for your voice," grumbled Steve. " Listen, then, you ungrateful; suspicious wretches! I did for you what no captain ever did for his men before. 1 hal exhausted all manner of disguises, so that the authorities would almost have looked for me in an old woman's gown ! See, then, what I did : I put myself on a month's regimen of vegetable diet, and kept myself in a cavern, until I grew as pale and thin as a hermit! Then I shaved off my hair, beard, moustaches and eye- brows I Yes, blame you, I sacrificed all my beauty to your interests! Fate helps those who help themselves. The camp-meeting gathering together hosts of people and preachers, gave me the oppor- tunity of appearing without exciting inquiry. I put on a grey wig, a black suit, assumed a feeble voice, stooping gait, and a devout manner, and-- became a popular preacher at the camp-meeting !" "Captain, you're a brick! you are indeed! I do not flatter you !" said Hal. It was a sentiment in which all agreed. "I had no need of further machination," con- tinued the captain: "they actually gave me the game! I was urged to visit you here—forced to remain alone and talk with you!" laughed Black Donald.

"And now, Captain my jewel! my treasure! my sweetheart! that I love with 'a love passing the love of woman!' how is your reverence going to get us out ?" Listen 1" said the Captain, diving into his pockets. "You must get yourselves out !—this prison is by no means strongly fastened, or well guarded. Here are files to file off your fetters; here are tools to pick the locks, and here are three loaded revolvers to use against any of the turnkeys who might discover and attempt to stop you. To- night, however, is the last of the camp-meeting, and the two turnkeys are among my hearers! I shall keep them all night! Now you know what to do. I must leave you. Dick, try to make an assault on me that I may scream—but first conceal your tools and arms." Hal hid the instruments, and Dick, with an awful roar, sprang at the visitor, who ran to the

c rating, crying: " Help ! help ! The warden came hurrying to the spot, "Take him out o' this, then, muttered Dick, sulkily, getting back into his corner. "Oh, what a wretch !" said Mrs. Condiment. "I shall be glad when he's once hanged," said ihe jailer. " I—l—fear that I can do them but little good, and—l would rather not come again, being sickly and nervous," faltered Father Gray. "No, my dear good sir. 1 for one shall not ask you to risk your precious health for such a set of wretches. They are Satan's own 1 You shall come home to our tent and lie down to rest, and I will make you an egg-caudle that will set you up again," said Mrs. Condiment, tenderly, as the whole party left the cell.

Thai. dawy the outrageous conduct of the im- prisoned burglars was the subject of conversation, even dividing the interest of the religious excite- ment.

But the next morning the whole community was thrown into a state of consternation by the dis- covery that the burglars had broken gaol and fled, and that the notorious outlaw, Black Donald, had been in their very midst, disguL.ed as an elderly field preacher.

CHAPTER XXIX. THE VICTORY OVER DEATH. 'Glory to God! to God!' he saith, 'Knowledge by suffering entereth, 'And life is perfected in death.'" --E. B. BRowNING One morning, in the gladness of his heart, Doctor Day mounted his horse and rode down to Staunton, gaily refusing to impart the object of his ride to any one, and bidding Traverse stay with the women until he should return.

As soon as the doctor was gone, Traverse went lit° the library to arrange his patron's books and papers. Mrs. Rocke and Clara hurried away to attend to some little mystery of their own invention, for the surprise and delight of the doctor and Traverse. F-r the more secret accomplishment. of their pur- pose, they had dismissed all attendance, and were at work alone in Mrs. Rocke's room. And here Clara's sweet, frank and humble disposition were again manifest, for when Marah would arise from her seat to get anything, Clara would forestall her purpose, and say: "Tell me—tell me to get what .von want, just as If I were your child, and you will make me feel so well-4o now !" "You are very good, dear Miss Clara, but—l would rather not presume to ask you to wait on me, said Marah, gravely. " Presume I what a word from you to me; please don't use it ever again, nor call me Miss Clara. Call me ' Clara ' or child,' do mamma," said the doctor's daughter ; then suddenly pausing, she blushed and was silent.

Mai•ah gently took her hand, and drew her into a warm embrace.

It was while the friends were conversing so kindly in Marah's room, and while Traverse was still engav,ed in arranging the doctor's books and papers, that one of the men-servants rapped at the library door, and without waiting permission to come in, entercd the room with every mark of terror in his look and manner.

" r.a,t i. he matter ? ' inquired Traverse, anxiously, rising. . .

'Oh, iNlr Traverse, sir! the doctor's horse has just rushed home to the stables all in a foam, with- out his rider.''

"Good Heaven !" exclaimed Traverse, starting up and seizing his hat ; "follow me immediately; hurry to The stables and saddle my horse, ani bring him tip instantly ! We must follow on the road the doctor took, to see what has happened 1 Stay ! on your life, breathe not a word of what has occurred ' I wo,ild not have Miss Day alarmed for the world l'' he concluded, hastening downstairs attended by the servant.

In five niinutes from the time he left the library, Trsaverse w•ts in the saddle, galloping towards Staunton, and looking attentively along the road as he went. Alas ! he had not gone far, when, in descending the wooded hill, he saw lying doubled up helplessly on the right side of the path, the body of the _good doctor!

11%i-di' an exclamation between a groan and a cry of anguish, Traverse threw himself from his saddle and kneeled beside the fallen figure, gazing in an agony of anxiety upon the closed eyes, pale features and contracted form, and crying: "Oh, Heaven have mercy I Doctor Day I oh, Doctor Day !—can you Teak to me ?" The white and quivering eyelids opened and the faltering tongue spoke: " Traverse —get me home—that I may see— Clara before I die."

"Oh, must this be so! must this be so I Oh, that I could die for you, my friend! my dear, dear friend I" cried Traverse, wringing his hands in such anguish as he had never known before. Then feeqng the need of self-control and the absolute necessity of removing the sufferer, Traverse repressed the swelling flood of sorrow in his bosom and cast about for the means of conveying the doctor to his house. He dreaded to leave him for an instant, and yet it was necessary to do so, as the servant whom he had ordered to follow him, had not yet come up. While he was bathing the doctor's face with water from a little stream beside the path, John, the groom, -came riding along, and seeing his fallen master, with an erclamation of horror, sprang from his saddle and ran to the spot. "John," said Traverse, in a heart-broken tone, "mount again and ride for rour life to the house ! have—a cart—yes! that will be the easiest convey- ance ! have a cart got ready instantly with a feather-bed placed in it, and the gentlest horse harnessed to it, and drive it here to the roadside at the head of this path. Hasten for your life ! say not a word of what has happened lest it should terrify the ladies! Quick! Quick! on your life!'

Again, as the man was hurrying away, the doctor spoke, faintly murmuring: "For heaven's sake—do not let—poor Clara be shocked !"

"No, no, she shall not be ; I warned him, dear friend. How do you feel ?—can you tell where you are hurt ?"

The doctor feebly moved one hand to his chest and whispered: "There, and in my back." Traverse, controlling his own great mental agony, did all that he could to soothe and alleviate the sufferings of the doctor, until the arrival of the cart that stopped on the road at the head of the little bridle-path where the accident happened. Tken John jumped from the driver's seat and came to the spot where he tenderly assisted the young man in raising the doctor and conveying him to the cart, and laying him upon the bed. Notwith- standing all their tender care in lifting and carry- ing him, it was but too evident that he suffered greatly in being moved. Slowly as they proceeded, at every jolt of the cart, his corrugated brows and quivering lips told how much agony he silently endured.

TSUCCESS AT LAST.—rfackleton " Moinec,nia me a bad coin yesterday, and I didn't know What to do with it." Von Blamer: " Couldn't you pass it?" Tackleton : "Not for a long time. The first man I tried it on was a ticket clerk, and he refused it with scorn." Von Blamer: "Why didn't you try it on a druggist? Their profits are so great they're likely to be careless in money matters.' Tackleton : "Oh'i I did; but it wasn't any use. Then I tried to buy a newspaper with t, but the boy wouldn't have it. I went in to buy a drink, but that wouldn't do. I was almost discouraged,• old man." Von Blamer: should say you might have been. But you finally passed it, didn't you?" Tackleton (proudly) : "Oh, yes !" Von Blamer : "On whom?" Tackleton : "On a friend of mine." How To MANAcin A DAUGHTER.—First—You can't do it; the man who can must be more than mortal. Second—Clive her her own way; it will save her the trouble of taking it. Third—Pay for her dresses if you can afford it. Her dressmaker will sue if you don't. Fourth—lf she takes a fancy to any man you don't want her to marry, tell her your heart is set on her marrying him, and swear she shall marry no other. You can then give her a free hand, and she wouldn't have him if he Was the only man left. Fifth—lf there is any man you want her to marry, kick him ont of your house, forbid the servants to admit him, distribute man- traps and spring guns and bulldogs all around your grounds, lock her in her room, and vow if she marries him you won't leave her a penny. You will not have to wait long after that for the enlope- ment. Sixth—lf she has no voice, encourage her to sing whenever you give a party. It will attract attention to her, and give your guests an excuse for complimenting her. Never mind the neigh- boars. Seventh—lf you are -a poor man, teach your daughter how to dance and play the piano. She can learn cooking and dressmakinc, and those thin,s after she is married.

WE MUST HAVE THE TOOLS.

Robinson Crusoe, you remember, made a big boat or canoe out of the trunk of a tree. It was a laborious and tedious job. And that wasn't the worst of it. When he got the boat done he couldn't launch it. It was too heavy for one man to handle. If he had only had so arrangement like the capstan of a ship he might haVe managed. He understood how to do it, but lacked the tools. How often we find ourselves at a dead stand for that same reason. Let me give you a fresh illustration, tied up for the moment in the following letter which must first be read before we can rightly come at the point.

In the spring of 1884," says our correspondent, " got into a low weak way, not being able to imagine what bad happened to me. My strength kept ebbing away till I had scarcely the desire or ability to do anything. I felt as tired as if I had just arrived home from a long, bard journey, yet no tax more than usual of any kind had been laid upon me. My mind, too, was weary; SO that I turned from things that obliged me to tbink,plan, or consider.

"Side by side, so to speak, with all this was the fai:ure of my appetite. Of course I continued to eat, or make an effort to eat,but food no longer tempted me aS it does a person in health. I picked and minced over my meals, and the little I took neither tasted good not did me any good after I had eaten it. Instead of warm- ing, comforting and stimulating me, as it used to do, it gave me distress at the stomach, pain at the chest, and a singular feeling of tightness around the waist, aS though a belt were buckled too snug around me. "After a time the condition of my stomach seemed to grow worse. There was that sense of gnawing, so often mentioned by others, and occasionally a feeling of faint. ness and sinking, almost like the ground giving %mg, under one's feet."

[Rinteßic : An eminent London physician, in oni: his books, describes this sinking feeling as one of kite most appalling and frightful that it is possible to experi- ence. It is not the body but the mind that suffers. I. the present writer, have had two attacks of it, and pray to. have ne more. It is like unto the overshadowing of the Death Angel's wing, with the mind fully conscious of the situation. The cause is uric acid poison in the blood, one of the producers of prolonged indigestion.) " When this sinking feeling came on," continues the letter, "it weighed me down like a nightmare. Finally I got to be so weak I could only walk slowly and feebly. The doctor who prescribed for me said my complaint %era dyspepsia, but his medicine had no perceptible effect. " I contin ed this for eight years ; not always the same, but now better and then worse. Yet in all that long time there was not a day when I could say I was well. No medicine or treatment seemed right for me. and I almost began to think I never should recover m 7 former health.

"In March, 1892, Mother S.igel's Syrnp was recom- mended to me as having done wonders in cases like mine, even when they were of long standing and every. thing else had failed. No harm to try it, we thought, ancrgot it from Mr. Grime, the chemist. in Bolton-road; and after taking it I felt great relief. My appetite quickly improved, and I could eat without pain. When I had taken two or three bottles more the bad symptomg had all gone, and I was as well as ever. My husband also took the medicine with the same results. You maY publish my letter and refer inquiries to me. (Signed) (Mrs.) Elizabeth Wilson, 5, Northcote-street, Bolton - road, Darwen, March Ist, 1895." The lesson in this interesting narrative is too plain for U 3 to miss it. Our old friend Crusoe was not able to launch his boat for the want of machinery. Similarlf the doctor who attended Mrs. Wilson was not able to cure her because he did not possess the right remedy. His opinion as to her complaint was entirely correct* She was suffering from chronic dysepsia, precisely as he told her. But alas! it is one thing to know what ought to be done and quite another to have the knoW- ledge and means to do it.

Between these two things (over this wide gap) stand!' Mother Seigel's Syrup, just as between the two sides of the Thames stands London:Bri4ge.

107 YEARS OLD.

Dr. William Salmon, of Penlynn crt Glamotgan shire (to whom last year Mr. Gladstone sent **sincere, wishes for here and hereafter"), has entered on his 107tu . . year. He was born in Suffolk in 1790, and moved with his father, also a doctor, to Cowbridge in 1798. He 111*° been for fifty years a magistrate for :Glamorganshire* and claims to be the oldest Fr emason in the world. He has had eight children, but only one, a daughter, is alive. He sleeps well, rises at noon, dines at six o'clock (when he takes two glasses of wine), and goes to bed at nine. He has not smoked since he was 90, nor has he been out for a drive since his 100th year.

The most stui),;(,):, ot wasting diseases, so-called, are %hose of either . sciolulous or a consumptive type. these diseases are closely related,being caused by germs. IS scrofula the germs are in the blood, and manifest therarelves in swellings and open sores, while in the trod common form of consumption the germs are seated le the lungs. Both diseases result from an impoveristt- bent of the'blood and the resulting loss of flesh, weak- ntss, and general emaciation.

It is difficult to say just what course the case we bear Put before you would have taken,and you can form your Own opinion of the nature of it from the symptoms ; but the most general application is to be found in the com- plete emaciation of the patient, which, without the rigte treatment, would have made recovery hopeless. THE JAPANESE INDUSTRIAL

INVASION.

(By MR. H. WHITHA3I.) (Continued from last week.)

A contemptuous optimistic indifference becomes a crime in the face of hard facts and 'figures such as may be found in most trade journals. 'Japanese exports for '1894 show an aggregate value of over noopoo England's merchants have voiced their alarm, and confess they have lost a large part of their Oriental cotton goods trade. British fabrics in China and other places in the Orient are being displaced by the fruits of the Japanese looms. I wish to impress it as strongly as possible upon the minds of my readers that it will be &fatal mistake to pooh,. pooh! the menace of Japanese competi- tion by assuming the people of Japan to be half civilized, and therefore not to be considered as rivals. Mr. Henry Norman in his beak "The real Japan," says, "The traveller in Japan soon learns to transfer his wonder from the individual to the nation. This extraordinary people are born with a manual dexterity that is simply astonishing. Any little jot that requires intelligence and manual skill almost any Japanese will do for you. Mr. Rein in his beok "The Ineustries of Japan,' says, "The eye and hand of the Japanese are on the average more practical than those of the European." Another writer in one of the English reviews tops the edifice by saying, "The Japanese possess attri- butes which have caused them to 'be rated as the most skilful on the globe." I assume, therefore, that it wotild be, the height of folly to say as has been already said, that : "To declare that a higher civilization has anything to fear from Commercial contact with nations on a lower plane of civilized efficiency is to impeach Civilization itself.' They combine with their artistic skill not only a great imitative faculty but also much inventive power where small artrconcentions and surprising effects are concerned. Sir Edwin Arnold declares that" the capacity-Of the Japanese for incrgaible detail in their work makes of them the most perfect workmen in the world,as they are the most rapid, and when one realizes that this ability and capacity can be obtained there for an average Of 44. a day against 6s. a day in America and 4s. a day and upwards in Englantl,it is not hard to see that there is reason in what ¶ say, that "the victory 6f*Japan over China may well make certain English !and American industries tremble for their future. It means that from now on cheap labor— labor which is incredibly cheap to us—will be thrown into competition with high-priced labor. Tariff duti, will not protect either country." The Asestican Consul General at Shanghai, says, "Japan realizes that she has a population well fitted for acquirement by skill of the technical manufac- turing arts, and has evinced her determination to manufacture not only for internal consumption, but for a broader market. She is aided in this under- taking by the fact that there is no protection of foreign patents. and artisans being gni& to imitate, she can successfully copy the best mechanical appli- ances of the countries of the globe, and can in this way produce a machine which for given work often excels any to be found in the wefld." Admiral Belknap, of the American Navy, writing to the New York' Sun " says, Japanese statesmen avow it to be their purpose to make Japan the England of tbe Pacific, sed the portentous events in the Orient, now commanding the startled attention of the world, are but the culmination of a 'long-cher- isbed policvand of maturedly planned pterposes.The island craft, the aggressive wisdom that has made England so dominant, so potential in the affairs of the globe for the past 300 years, is fast finding its vigorous counterpart in the progressive steps and well-considered aims of Japan." We all know the old legend about the secret of England's greatness, we all, or most of us, know now that her greatness has been the outcome Of the mental and physical faculties of her sons, conibined with the rare good fortune which gave her the -first use of the steam engine. Labour has determined 'her growth. England had half won before any other 'nation had started in the race. That higher wages mere paid in England than in any other European country was because of the absence of competition. to soon as Germany. Belgium and other countries entered into the struggle the whole situation Wag changed,and now it is a life and death struggle,even tothe cannon's mouth, for ne.w,and still newer mar- kets. The English w Tkrnan now finds that the same law rules him that. rules every other sociolog- ical problem,—it is the survival of the fittest We have seen, more than once, the English workman trying, and trying in vain to form international trades onions. The idea remains as yet a mere dream. Each nation for itself. So Japan having the qualities, as acknowledged by all writers, necessary to industrial, and commercial success is now using cheap labour as the fulcentn upon which she intends to rise as an industrial nation. A leading British trade journal, the " Textile Mercury " of March last, speaking on the general question says, "Increasing the hears of labour or lessening the wages of her (t43ngland's) mill operatives are alternatives that might stem the tide for a few yearfs'perhaps.but the systero'of labour oganization which prsvrtils in England is too strong to render this possible," and we seem to be fully warranted in saying," that it is high time the English cotton

===== 0002601_18960321_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
Ispinner aad manufacturer should look out for suit- able fields to which he may emigrate." ollf.; is tempted to ask— what about the poor workman who has done so much towards creating the emi- grating capital ? I.Consul-generalAlelvor of the United States State Department gives the following average of wages which he brought to a gold basis by determining the value of the silver yen on the date the computa- tion of the rate of wages was made. JAPANESE WAGES. Cts. Cts. per per Day. D Ay. Carpenters Plasterers 26 Printers Mat'ing-makers.. Screenmakers 26 Laborers Tailors CAton-beaters Porcelain-makers Lacquer-workers One naturally asks, how do they live? Well, having sat at the table more than once with the Jap, I at least can testify to the fact that they know ho* to clean their trenchers. But in Japan, well, a workingman can furnish his house there for less than £2. The Jap uses only matting, and he carpets his house of two or threerooms for as many dollars. His household pots and pans may cost him nearly 63. A couple may go into comfortable house- keeping for about .£l. His rent will be about 4d. a week. He rises at 6 o'clock and has for breakfast rice and tea. The rice is often cold, and it may have been left over from last night's dinner. He pours hot tea upon it to warm it and eats it with chop sticks. At 8 o'clock he begins work,and at neon he has a lunch of rice generally furnished by his em- ployer. At 6he eats his dinner at home. This con- sists of plenty of rice, a little dried fi-,h, and clams if they are in season. The standard of living among them:is decidedly very poor. So much for living and 'wanes. Before I deal in more detail with the various in- dustries which are likely to be, in the near future, important factors in the competitive struggle, must refer briefly to 'Lord Rosebery's idea that Japan will be a wealthy customer of England as soon as her affairs are settled. This idea was voiced by A. Doisy, the founder of the Japan society in London,and published in the British Trades Journal early in 1895. "The enormous development of the cotton spinning industry which lb sure to follow the war will necessitate the importation from Eng- land of very large quantities of textile maahinery. Ship building in Japan will receive a very great impetus ! That, again, will be tor the benefit of British trade.which must supply the accessories re- quired for that industry, such as all kinds of machine tools and fittings. Then, with the revival of trade generally, the chemical trade will feel the benefit, and there should be a large increase in the exports of chemicals to Japan for all kinds of in- dustries, dyeing, tanning, match making and others. Glass will be wanted, and that glass will come from abroad. Hats, boots, shoee and clothing ,of all kinds will be wanted." This is a sample of I the great trade that is going to be done with Eng- land and America. Is it satisfactory? I fear not. The export of .machinery, and skilled labour. The British- machinists while making the machinery may well say" after us the deluge." Now let us see what is really being done in the manufacture of cotton textiles. This industry is the leading one so far introduced in Japan, and Sir Edwin Arnold writing last April from London to an American paper says" I believe that Japan will ere long play very hot with Manchester, our great English cotton manufacturing centre?' The cotton spinning indus+ry of Japan has made rapid Arides, and the number of spindles has reached 1,000,000. Some of the spinners advise care in starting new mills, thinking it rather risky, as there will be many difficulties in the way of obtain- ing raw cotton and maintaining the equilibrium of supply and demand.ln 1883 there were• 43,704 spin- dles. This is an immense development in 12 years' time, and Japan is not only supplying her home consumption, but is shipping cotton fabrics abroad. Speaking of this phenomenal development the Britishirrade Journal of London says :—" We must attribute the exceptional state of affairs in Japan to theenergy and foresight of its people? In the course of a few years no less than -59 cotton spinn- ing mills and weaving factories have sprung into existence there with upwards of .£4,000,000 of native capital. In May last it was estimated that their annual output was over 500,000 bales of yarn, valuediroughly at £8,000,000. The development of this industry has, no doubt, affected the exports of yarn and cloth from Lancashire and India. A re- port recently issued by the Foreign Office of the U.S., says :—" The mills and weaving sheds have made an average profit of over 17 per cent." A correspondent of the London" Times" writes: —" The rapid growth of the Japanese industry, and the results already achieved are undoubtedly cal- culated to strike the imagination with astonish- ment and alarm. In 1885 Japan imported only £160,090 worth of raw cotton. In 1894 she imported £3,990000 worth, or more than four-and-twenty times as much." In 1888 Japan imported from Great Britain and India, in about equal proportions, the total amount of £62,860,000. Six years later, in 1894 the importation from the salae countries amounted only to .X21,241,000,0r barely one third of the former figures. If the pinch has not yet been felt mora severely in England, it is due to the fact that the loss has so far fallen more heavily on Bombay than upon Lancashire, for while the imports from the latter have been reduced 40 per eent.,those from the former have suffered to the extent of $0 per cent. Last year she sent .£450,000 worth of cotton goods to China. It must be quite clear that the <question is a serious one. For climatic reasons the Oriental marketis an almost illimitable one, and it is poor comfort to say that "English manufacturers of machinery may profit by providing the a-awes° with tte means of undoing Lancashire?' A curious feature is that America sells raw cotton to Liver- pool and 'London, which is re-shipped to Japan, manufactured and exported as finished fabric to America. The London Economist of February 17th, 1894, presented-the balance sheets of ninety-three spinn- ing companies of Oldham showing a net total loss of £72,768 in 1893. In 1873, according to Consuls reports, the mills of the Orient anti Occident were competing on equal terms and receiving equal re- turns. Now, in 1894, each mill employs the same amount of labor as it did in 1873 but the owner of the mill in -the United States pays for the labor in gold at the old rates, while the owner of the mill in Japan pays 'for labor in silver at the old rates also. The Japanese mill owner paid in 1894, as he did in 1873, from It to'2o cents a day for men, and from 8 to 10 cents a day for women. Now, during the greater part of 1894 one dollar in geld has been equal to 2 dollars in Japanese silver,which makes it clear that on aceount of the depreciation of silver atone, without taking into account the low rate of wages paid in the Orient the millowners of the Occident n a are paying twice as much for labor a the mill- owners of Japan. This may be one reason why the the States 5 a cottonwhlei n millst h n anred s how Greatin gßs Britain goodreturns ey ar are struggling for a bare existence. Mr. T. H. Whitehead in an address delivered be- fore the Royal Colonial Institute in 1896 said.—" So long as the gold value of silver continues to be as it now is, liable to violent fluctuations, the more peri- lous must become the condition of the principal British industries, and the more impossible must it be to prevent disaster from overtaking them. British labor and gold capital can no longer compete on equal terms with Asiatic labor and silver capital, and the position of British industries is growing more critical every day." I The Japanese successfully compete with the Eng- lish in Bombay, where there are eighty of the largest and best equipped mills in the world. The largest mill in Bombay, equipped with the finest machinery and all other modern improvements, employs 4,200 hands and turns out 45,000 pounds of manufactured goods pet day. The average wages paid to hands, skilled and unskilled including the manager and office force is 13 rupees a month, or about 14s. in English money. Last year Bombay exported 125,000,000 pounds of cotton goods and I 54,000,000 yards of piece goods to compete with I Japanese products, while she imported from Great Britain 2,400,000,000 yards of cloth for her own con- sumption. At the business meeting held in San Francisco, and to which I have referred, a letter sent by Japanese capitalists was read urging the California Cotton Mills Company to move its plant to the Orient in which the writer said as an inducement, "You need only bring over a few of your skilled workmen, and those but for a month. Our people will easily learn the work in that time." I It is not only with cotton that Japan threatens to invade the West but in other ways to which I will draw attention in another article.

THE FXAMINER, SATURDAY, MARCH 21, 1896.

AN ESSEX SAMARITAN.

The Woo'bridge Reporter publishes an interesting ac- went of "The Risex Samaritan," more exactly known as Mr. George Edwards, of Little Bealings,near Ipswich, who, though a quiet and retiring person, is noted locally for the good he has done to people who have had the benefit ot his advice, wnich we now give to our readers. It appears that Mr. Eiwards' lore is the result of hiS own experience:

" When I was a boy." said he," I had a bad attack of rheumatic fever, and another about eleven years ago, when I was laid up for twenty-one weeks." • " And up to two years ago," interrupted his wife. "if he got a c4d or the weather wes changeable he suffered most exsruciating pains in all his limbs. He could get no rest night nor day, for he could not sleep at night nor rest in the day-time."

"Yes," continued Mr. Edwards, '66 it was about two years ago, just before Christmas, when I was suffering dreadfully with rheumatism in my legs, so that I could not get my stockings on, nor walk. Nothing did me any good. One day I was looking Ttt the newspaper and saw what wonderful things Dr, Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People had done, and E said 6 have a try at them.' So I sent right away to Dr. Williams' place for a box."

Mrs. Edwards again interposed here : " Yes " she said, " and after he had taken that one box he felt great re- lief, and so he sent again and got another." " I had only three boxes altogether," continued the husband. "And that properly cured him," chimed in the wife emphatically.

"I have never had a rheumatic pain since, and I tell everybody how much good they have done me; and this had been the means, as you have heard, .of great benefit to many people who have been led to use Dr. Williams' Pink Pills by my example." Mr. Edwards' disinterested story carries conviction on tha surface. and confirms reports from all quarters of the remarkable cures effected by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, not only-in cases of rheumatism, but in all the many disorders which follow influenza and rheumatic fever, or arise from an impoverished state of the blood and nerves, such as anvemia, pale and sallow complexion, general muscular weakness, loss of appetite, shortness of breath, pains in the back, nervous headache, early decay, all forms of female weakness, hysteria, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, sciatica, palpitations, low vitality, and wasting strength from any cause. These Pills are genuine only with the full name, Dr. Williams' Pink pills for Pale People, and are sold by chemists and by Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, 46, Holborn Viaduct, London, E.G., at 23. 91. a box, or six for 133. 9J. CYCLING FOR LADIES.

Of all forms of femiaine athletics the most popular at I the present day is undoubtedly cycling. Whether it be I but the passiug craze of the hour or whether it has " come to stay," as our American friends would term it, it is impossible to decide. probably its many advan- tages of a means of rapid and easy progression will always recommend it ta ladies until a flying machine or some such novelty is invented. At present, however,the demand for ladies' cycles is so great that the makers find it difficult to keep up the supply. The Lsndon parks are crammed with the fair enthusiasts, who number amongst them representatives of every grade of society. Makers who could read the signs of the times invented long ago a special machine for ladies' use. In 1893 all Paris went mad after the new craze, but the gay Parisiennes produced such wonderful " creations " in the way of rational dress that the movement in England was rather retarded than advanced. Last year however, cycling sprang into sudden popularity amongst English ladies. It became the " proper thing "to do, and the lady cyclist at last felt her position assured. From royalty downward everyone seemed to have elognt the cycling fever, and that severely. In fact, 1895 will probably be known in the future as the "ladies year." Battersea Park was the great rendezvous. There ladies rode at a moderate pace, met their friends, and extracted just that amount of enjayment out of the exercise which was possible without over-exerting them- selves. The presence of the fair riders soon collected large and fashionable crowds, and Tuesday and Thursday mornings in Battersea Park soon became re- cognised society functions. It would be impossible to enumerate the names of half the wellirnown ladies who were to be seen at the cycle promenades,amongst whom were Lady Dudley, Lady Wolverton, Lady Lurgan, and Lady Norreys. This year Hyde Park has been opened to cyclists, and this convenient field of operations has still further increased the number of lady cyclists. On a fine morning the mnving crowd of pretty faces and dainty dresses make a charming spectacle. Occasionally the old and new styles meet,and the spectacle. which can- net fail to prove amusing to old Park habitues, is presented of a damsel on her iron steed accompanied by a cavalier on horseback. Accidents are rare, for the bicycle " scorcher "is conspicuous by his absence. If he does put in an appearance, the police " keep their eye on him" pretty closely. The latest recruit to royalty on wheels is Queen Margherita of Italy. An amusing story is told of an interview between the Queen and a sentry, who, in ignorance of her identity, was protesting against her crossing the royal sward of the Park Monza. The Queen, who had just had a tumble, replied merrily, "I may do what I like with mine own, but I find that mine own sometimes does what it likes with me." Princess Maud of Wales has lately become an enthusiastic cyclist, and has been photog,raphed on her machine in company with her fiancé. Princess Victoria of Wales has now ridden far same time. On the vexed question of rational dress it is difficult for a mere man to sneak. It would appear, however, that society has set its face sternly against its introduction. It is scarcely ever seen in the London parks. On the other hand, the more energetic and enthusiastic lady cyclists, who believe in long tours in the country, have in most cases adopted it. It is cer- tainly safer, and seems more appropriate when long rides in a strong wind or heavy rain may have to be under- taken. At the same time, a shortish skirt, well cut and stiffened, is far less an encumbrance than many ladies who do not cycle would imagine. However, the whole I,question is a problem, the solution of which only the I future can show.—" Cassell's Family Magazine."

Mr. Ballington Booth has named the secessionist sec- tion of the Salvation Army " God's American Volun- teers," and he announces that all official relations with his father are now at an end.

THE NAVIGATION OF THE MERSEY.

CONSERVATOR'S REPORT.

THE SHIP CANAL AND THE RIVER CHANNEL&

The report of Admiral Sir G. 11. Richards, the acting conservator, on the state of the navigation of the river Mersey during 1895 gives some remark- able figures as to the work accomplished at the "bar ' e the Queen's Channel. The quantity of sand removed from the bar during the first six months was 1,346,820 tons. The Brancker also re- moved during the same period 1,105,300 tons of sand from the spit of Taylor Bank on the east side of the ship channel. During the latter half of the year the work was carried on at the Queen's Channel and in the vicinity of Taylor Spit, as cir- cumstances would permit. The weather during this period was not on the whole favourable for dredging. The new 3,000-ton sand-pump dredger, G. B Crow, a duplicate of the Braneker as regards power and capacity,vvas received from the Barrow Ship- building Company in the late autumn, and commenced work on the bar on the 25th of November. She removed 221,350 tons of sand up to the end of the year. The quantity of sand removed during the last six months was 1,459,150 tons, making a total from the commence- ment of the dredeing operations in the month of Septem- ber, le9o, of 11,741,950 tons from the bar. From the west end of Taylor Spit there has been removed since the commencement of the work 2,600,550 tons. The ' general result f the dredging operations on the bar dur- ing the year 1895 cannot but be regarded as very satis- factory. The minimum depth at dead low water of spring tides is 22 feet,and that only in a few spots on the out- side line of the cut. The maximum depth is 29 feet, and the mean (repth 25 feet ; thus the floor is not yet by any means unit orm,but in mid-channel, and for a width of 500 feet.a depth of 24 feet can be carried at low water springs. The Liverpool Dock and Harbour Board being now in possession of two powerful sand-pump dredgers, each of 3,000 tons carrying power,and capable.together, of removing in an ordinary day about 30,000 tons of sand, I am of opinion that there need be no apprehension of any deterioration in the depths of water on the bar, but, on the contrary,that the necessary navigable depths may be easily maintained. Concerning the Manchester Ship Canal,. Admiral Richards says :—During the year 1895 dredging opera- tions have been carried out for deepening the approach to the canal entrance at gastham. By section 28 of the Act of 1885 the Canal Company are empowered to dredge this channel to a depth not exceeding 20 feet below the datum level of old dock sill, which means there will he a depth of 10 feet in the channel at low water of a 21- feet tide. Under the powers of the Canal Act of 1894 a series of timber dolphins have been constructed on either side of the channel below Eastham for the convenience of ships entering the canal, viz., thirteen on the eastern and four on the western side. Three smaller mooring dolphins have also been constructed above the locks on the western bank of the land. _ _ _

As to the upper waters of the canal, it was stated in my last annual report that the portion of the River Mersey between Runcorn and Warrington had consider- ably deteriorated, the primary cause, or at any rate the one which is remediable, being, in my opinion, the loss of scour caused by the diversion of the waters of the Mersey and other rivers from their original course, and the failure of the works provided for by the Act of 1885 to fulfil their intention, viz., the return of the greater portion of the waters so divided from the canal to the river at certain points below. It was stated also that the Canal Company were undertaking works in the hope of remedying this condition of things, to form a correct idea of which it may be stated that for a length of about eleven miles of the upper portion of the canal, or from Manchester to Rixton Junction' which is about three miles from Latchford Locks (the first locks in the canal, where there is a rise of 16 feet), the canal is the only flood course for the waters of the rivers Irwell and Mersey, and other smaller streams on the watershed, the Mersey itself having been severed seven miles below Manehester, and its course turned into the canal. At Rixton Junction two courses are available for these waters, first the original course of the Mersey, which passes through Warrington into the upper estuary, and secondly the straight length of the canal, which they have preferred to the former tortuous course to such an extent that during the floods of the autumn the tops of the locks at Latchford have been overflowed from two to three feet,and the immediate neighbourhood partially flooded.

Prier to the construction of the canal three weirs existed in the former course, viz., Butchersfield, Wools- ton, and Rowley. and in order to facilitate the passage of floods through this channel into the estuary the works before alluded to have recently been executed, viz. : The old river channel from its junction with the canal at Rixton to the eastern end of the Butchersfield Cut has been dredged to a uniform depth of about ten feet below ordinary water level. The masonry of the old bridges at the eastern end and that the locks at the western end of this cut have been entirely removed,and the cut widened and deepened,and the Butchersfield Weir,which was 165 feet wide,ha,s been removed so as to give a minimum depth of six feet over I the entire width.

Woo!sten Weir has been entirely removed, and 16 tilt- ing sluices, each lOtt, in width, have been constructed in its place. These sloices 'tan be opened in such man- ner as to present a minimum obstruction to the passage of the water in times of flood, and have been found to a height sufficient to pertnk of the level of the water in the Latchford pond of thecanal being carried an with- out interruption to Woolston. No alterations have been made at Howley Weir, which remains, with its sluices and caps, as it was prior to the construction of the canal. I am informed by Mr. Hunter, the Canal Company's engineer, that since the partial completion of these works strong freshes have been discharged by their moans into the upper estuary, the sluices at Latchford Locks not having been raised at any part of the flood. It will have been seen, however, that the upper part of the sluices have been very con- siderably flooded on at least two occasions; Mr. Hunter, remarks that in November and December quantities of , water, at the rate of 500,000,000 cubic feet in 24 hours, were calculated to have passed through the new works. What effect these operations may have on the river remains to be seen, but the following memorandum, v.hich is the result of the observations of the Liverpool Dock awl Harbour Board's marine surveyors during the year 1895, will show its condition at that time : A comparison of the survey of April, 1895, with that of the same time in 1884 shows that the tidal capacity of that portion of the river Mersey between Runcorn and Walton bridges (Warrington) has diminished in some portions, while in ethers it has increased. On the whole the tidal capacity appears to have increased by 110,254 cubic yards. The increased capacity must in a great measure be attributed to the dredging which has been carried out by the Manchester Skip Canal Company above Bank Quay, under their Parliamentary obligations. Between Randall sluices and Bank Quay, the tidal capacity of the river has been diminished by 154,583 cubic yards and in consequence probably of the dredg- ing above Bank Quay, just mentioned. B-tween Runcorn Bridge and Randall _sluices there

apoears to have been an increase of tidal capacity of 117.137 cubic yards ; forming, as it were, a basin be- tween narrower channels above and below, it may,under ordinary circumstances, be expected to undergo con- siderable changes. The undermentioned places, which before the opening _ . _ _ _ _

of the Ship Canal were habitually used by flats drawing from 7ft. to 9ft. of water for anchorage at low water, and where they could lie without taking the ground, have shoaled to such an extent, that they can no longer be used for that purpose. viz. : " Fidler's Ferry." On the Lancashire side, about eti miles above Runcorn Bridge. "Richmond Lay-by." On the Lancashire side about threequarters of a mile above Fidler's Ferry.. "Thorn Hedge." On the Cheshire side at the bottom of Bank Quay. " Reach Bar."

N) other anchorages or lay-by places of the same kind as those above-mentioned have developed themselves. En those parts of the river where there is deep water the deep channels are much narrower than they formerly were, and the general condition of the river for the pur- poses of navigation has become much worse than it was before the Ship Canal was made. The deterioration is believed to have been caused by the largely diminished passage of fresh water down the river, consequent on its having been diverted from its natural course and passed down the canal. From the subjoined statement it will be seen that there is reason to apprehend that the mean tidal height in the upper part of the Mersey, of the tides rising more more than 206 at George's Pier, Liverpool, is being reduced.

THEY MADE A. MISTAKE.

The number of peoplc in this world who are basing their entire action and shaping their whole lives upon mistaken ideas is almost incalculable. A wrong theory will destroy the best matured and most carefully con- ducted scheme, and it will also ruin the the most earnest and sincere life. The world is full of misanthropic people who have become embittered as the result of mis- taken ideas. For years the world believed that rheu- matism was merely a muscular disorder which could be removed by the liberal use of liniments. It is now a established and acknowledged fact that the Rheumatism and Gout are blood diseases due to an excess of uric acid in the blood, and that there is only one successful treatment, which is to purify the blood by the remov• ing the excess of uric acid. THE CHILDHOOD OF THE EARTH.

As our earth is daily losing internal heat, or the equivalent of heat, it must have c*ntained more heat yesterday than in does to-day, more last year than this year, more twenty years ago than ten years ago. The effect has not been appreciable in historic times ; but when we rise from hurldreds of years to thousands of years,from thousands of years to hundreds of thousands of years, and from hundreds of thousands of years to millions of years, the effect is not only appreciable, but even of startling magnitude. There must have been a time when the earth contained much more heat than at present. There must have been a time when the surface of the earth was sensibly hot from this source. We cannot pretend to say how many thousands or millions of years ago this epoch was; but we may be sure that earlier still the earth was even hotter,until at length we seem to see the temperature increase to a red heat, from a red heat we look back to a still earlier age when the earth was white hot, back again till we find the surface of our now solid globe was actually molten. We need not push the retrospect any further at present, still less is it necessary for us to attempt to assign the probable origin of that heat. This, it will be observed, is not required in our argument. We find heat now,and we know that heat is being lost every day. From this the conclusion that we have already drawn seems ' inevitable, and thus we are conducted back to some remote epoch in the abyss of time past when our solid earth was a globe molten and soft throughout.—From "The Story of the Heavens."

SHIP CANAL. LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

Ths sea-going traffic upon the Manchester Ship Canal last week was the largest recorded for some time past. As compared with the returns for the preceeding seven days there was an increase of five ships and 7,252 regis- tered tons. Of this increase 5,917 tons were foreign traffic, and 1,335 tons coastwise. Amongst the imports were fifteen cargoes of general merchandise, nine of stone, eight of pig iron, two of woodpulp, two of china 'flay, and one of fruit. Two large cargoes of pyrites were discharged by the steamers Stakesby and Blythe. wood, from Huelva, and the Lady Mostyn, a steamer of 1,467 tons, brought a full cargo of iron ore to Ellesmere Port from La Laja. Other notable imports were 18,000 bags of flour from Fiume; 34,285 bushels of grain from Atherica, and 6,438 bales of cotton from America and Egypt. Twenty two general cargoes were exported, in- cluding one to China ; and seven of coal, including one to St. Thomas ; salt was shipped to Bahia and Aberdeen, pitch to Les Sables d'Olonne, bricks to Belfast, and con- tractors' plant to Devenport. Appended is an approxi- mate statement of the traffic for the week ended March 13 :—lnwards : Coastwise, 35 ships, 8,714 net tonnage; toreiF,,n, 15 shins, 10,452 net tonnage; total, 50 ships, 19,166 net tonnage ; Outwards :Coastwise, 47 ships, 11,388 net tonnage; foreign, 16 ships, 9,990 net tonnage; total, 63 ships, 21,378 net tonnage.
