THE SALVATION ARMY. GENERAL BOOTH ON THE AMERICAN SECESSIONS.

Under the auspices of the Salvation Army, a great demonstration was held at the Crystal Palace on Mon- day to celebrate the return home of General Booth. The secession of Mr. Ballington Booth and his followers was much discussed. The spirit of obedience and unity was emphasised by the conspicuous display of balners,bear- log, among others, the following inscriptions :—" Wel- come to our only General," " One Kingdom, one flag," and" One army, one General." General Booth in his address to a vast throng in the central transept, dealt mainly with the secession of his son. He had returned, he said, from his travels loyal to the old flag. By that be meant the objects and the principles and the methods of the yellow, red, and blue ensign. Those principles were written, as it were, on the very skies, and had been sworn to by tens of thousands of men and women. He himself hoped to be true to that flag to the end. They might then place it on his coffin, and bear testimony to the fact that he had always been loyal to the Salvation Army. Continuing, the Gerrral said he had returned7to this country to meet one of the heaviest and one of the bitterest trials he had over been called upon to face. But even the darkness of this trial had its silver lining. His Americam Army stood almost to a man true to him, and assurances of unceasing devotion had poured in upon him from every quarter of the globe. The result of the present trouble would, he anticipated, be that the bonds of union between the various sections would be drawn closer, and he was preparing for the dispatch of rein- forcements to the United States—(Applause.) Some per- sons had questioned the wisdom of certain of his deci- sions. Although he had no claim to infallibility, he claimed to know something of his own business, and he pointed to the Salvation Army in the four quarters of the glabo as a proof of the correctness of the judgments he had formed.—(Loud applause.)

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

MONDAY.-THE ADVANCE INTO THE SOUDAN.

In the :louse of Commons on Monday Mr. BRODRICK, replying to a question put by Mr. S. Gedge, said that the Duke of Cambridge when the Commander-in-Chief drew £6,631. 14-s. 2d. a year for pay and for age allow- ance, including his pAy as c@lonel of the Grenadier Guards, to which position he was appointed in 1857. Since he retired the Duke had only received £2,200 from army funds, due to him in respect of his colonelcy. In addition to his pay the Duke had, of course, received £12,000 a year granted to him in 1850. Mr. P. WILLIAMS in answer to a question from Colonel Russell, said that about £5OO worth of forage, of a kind not procurable ha this country,was obtained in Germany for the use of the Ashanti expedition._ .

Mr. BRODRICK informed Sir W. Lawson that the troops were not given permission to pillage when they entered Coomassie. In reply to Sir A. Rollit, Mr. Curzon said the dovera- ment had received official proposals from the United States Government with regard to the Venezuelan question, which were now the subject of negotiation. He was, however, unable to depart from the rule that negiota- tions should not be discussed in Parliament while they were going on. _

Sir W. HARCOURT asked whether the Government proposed to make the House any statement with refer- ence to the advance of troops in the Valley of the Nile. Mr. CunzoN said that for some weeks past the Govern- ment bad received rumours of contemplated advances by the dervish forces in Upper Egypt, threatening three separate objectives. Considerable anxiety was very naturally felt at this serious news,the more so as already at that time the Italian forces were known to be in diffi- culties upon the shores of the Red Sea. Then came the unfortunate event at Adowa, a disaster which was at- tended with a double danger. It was felt that immedi- ate action was required, and an advance had been ordered to Akasher, which was on the Nile and about one-third of the distance between Wady Haifa and Don- gola. The British advance might ultimately extend to Dongola,but in any case the future action of the Govern- ment must be regulated by considerations not merely military and strategical, but also political and financial. The Government hoped that the effects of the advance w-iuld be twofold—that on the one hand it would aid the Italians, and that it might in the second place save Egypt from a menace which, if left to grow, might swell to most formidable dimensions.— Mr. LABOUCHERE asked leave to move the adjournment of the House, and, leave beinc, given, proceeded to urge that the Government, instead of advancing up the Nile, ought to take steps to fulfil the pledges we had given to evacuate Egypt. Sir C. MUER seconded the motion. Sir W. HARCOURT said that if the movement ordered by the Government were really the first step in a policy of advance, it was a policy of the most perilous character, and one which the party on his side of the House would meet with the most steadfast resistance. Mr. COURTNEY said that if this was a movement to resume the conquest of the Soudan it must be condemned. After further debate Mr. Labouchere's motion for the adjournment was defeated by 268 votes to 126.

The House afterwards went into Committee of Supply on the Army Estimates, and Mr. Brodrick,in making the usual statement, said the total of the Estimates was £18,056,000. After comparing the cost of our army with that of Germany, he went on to say that the Gov- ernment proposed to slowly increase the reserve of cor- dite, to arm the Volunteers with the magazine rifle,and, where the Volunteers were absolutely unable to do it themselves, to provide ranges where all the forces in the district could practice.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday Mr. BRODRICK stated that an application had been received by the Secretary of State for War for permission to raise a cavalry regiment "on certain conditions," but some difficulty was felt in accepting the services of such a corps, and the application was declined. Replying to Mr. Dilziel,Mr. A. J. BALFOUR said it was true that the Government had changed their minds with regard to the allowance to the Duke of Cambridge since August last. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN informed Mr. Galloway that Presi- dent Kruger had been distinctly told that the Govern- ment would not consent to discuss any modification of article 4 of the London Convention of 1884. Mr. CURZON, in response to a request from Mr. J. Morley, read the telegrams and despatches which had induced the Government to order the advance up the Nile to Akasher.

Mr. J. H. Wu-sox brought before the House the article referring to, him in the previous day's " St. James's Gazette," and abked the House, in its own interests, to appoint a committee to inquire into the truth os other- wise of the charges made against him. Mr. BALFOUR said it did not appear to him that the Hulse was a fitt- ing body to appoint a tribunal to rehear a case which had already been brought before one of the ordinary tribunals. There was na response to Mr. Wilson's appeal for the appointment of a" Committee of Honour," and the House proceeded to the next business. Mr. H. WHITELEY proposed a resolution expressing the opinion that "the instability of the relative value of gold and silver since the action of the Latin Union in 1873 has proved injurious to the best interests of this country," and urging the Government to do all in its power to secure by international agreement a stable monetary par of exchange between gold and silver. Sir W. H. HonmswonTn seconded the motion. The Chan- cellor of the Exchequer (Sir M. HICKS-BEACH) made a statement, in the course of which he declared that the , Government were prepared to join in a conference to I consider the evils arising from the fluctuations in the respective value of gold ond silver, but they were not prepared to interfere with the currency of this country. The resolution was eventually put and carried without a division.

In the House of Lords the Earl of Rosebery put severa questions to the Prime Minister with regard to the Soudan Expedition. Lord Salisbury declined to produce the telegrams from Egypt. The military authorities, with Lord Cromer's approval, recommended an advance "towards the dervishes for the double purpose of check- ing them and helping to relieve Kassala." Later in the sitting it was pointed out to the Premier that the tele- grams which he refused to produce bad been read to the House of Commons by Mr. Curzon. The noble Marquis thereupon read the three telegrams which Mr. Curzon had read to the House of Commons. They were, he explained, " selected."

WEDNESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Wednesday Mr. BAILEY moved the second reading of the Poor-law Officers Superannuation Bill. He said the Act passed in 1864, being permissive, had operated very harshly in the case of many deserving officers where the guardians declined to avail themselves of it. The present bill was intended to remedy that defect. Captain NORTON seconded the motion. Mr. H. C. PrxrixErr asked that Ireland might be included in the bill, and Mr. Bailey assented. Mr. Looss objected to the measure on the ground that it sought to compel boards of guardians to pension their officials with money taken out of the local rates. He moved the rejection of the bill. Mr. J. BURNS seconded the amendment. After some further discussion, Mr. Logan withdrew his amendment, and the bill was read a second time and then referred to a Standing Commit- I tee on Law. The second reading of the Agricultural Produce (Marks) Bill was moved by Mr.Munisev. He explained that the object of the bill was to secure that all foreign and colonial meat exposed for sale should bear a label or mark to that effect, and that every vendor of such meat should have in a conspicuous position in his shop a sign with the words" dealer in foreign and colonial meat." Every dealer in such meat would have to be registered in a register kept by the local authority, and the register would be open to inspection. The rejection of the bill was moved by Mr. Parker-Smith.Mr. RITCNIE, who denied that the bill was intended to lead to Protee- tion, suggested that it should be read a second time and referred to a Select Committee. Mr. BRYCE regarded the bill as substantially Protectionist in its character. After further debate Mr. Mildmay moved the closure. 'I he Speaker accepted the motion, which was carried by 224 votes to 98 The motion for the second reading was carried by 239 votes to 82, but on the motion to refer the bill to a Select Committee objection was taken, and tbe motion stood over.

The deaths included-1 from 'typhoid fever, 4 from phthisis, 1 from cancer, 1 from goitre, 1 from old age, 5 from lung disease, 6 from brain disease. 2 from heart disease.

MARVELLOUS BARGAINS IN GENUINE SECOND-HAND FURNITURE, PIANOS, &c., T.OW, DOVESTON, SNOWDON. DYSON, OGDEN, LAMB,

of 1,200 men. This was the most formidable of the attacks made by the Soudanese upon the Egyptian frontiers in recent years, yet it was easily repelled. It was certainly a very striking confirmation the opinion of those military men who held that Wady Haifa was a practically im- pregnable frontier line. It will be seen therefore that as far as military reasons go there is little to be said for a further advance up the Nile. It is of course alleged by the Government that move- ments are reported to be taking place among the Mandists which may possibly portend an attack upon Egypt, but clearly that is in itself no argu- ment for leaving a strong defensive position for a position which can be more easily attacked. On the whole therefore, it is a reasonable conclusion that the present expedition is really dic- tated by political motives. What are those motives? We notice that in some quarters it is suggested:that the expedition has been planned by the Government in order to delay the long- , promised evacuation of Egypt by British troops. ' It is pointed out that once the re-c4nquest of the Soudan is entered upon the evacuation question is indefinitely postponed, for the Egyptian Govern- ment could not by its own unaided resources , carry out such an enterprise. Such an explana- tion as this is in the highest discreditable to the Government, and the country generally will be very reluctant to believe that any body of English statesmen could adopt a scheme so underhanded and truly Machiavellian in order to avoid the neces- sity of dealing openly and candidly with an inter- national obligation. The re-conquest of the Soudan would, looked at merely as a mili- tary enterprise, be an extremely serious undertaking. It would probably mean the destruction of many thousands of human lives and the wasting of an immense amount of treasure,and in the end the onerous ae.d profitless 'obligation of administering the affairs of these arid and bar- barous regions would be thrown upon the British Government. In addition to this the hostility of France—and in these matters France is backed by Russia--7might easily be roused to an extent that would endanger the peace of the world. When all these circumstances are taken into account it is difficult to believe that the re-conquest of the Soudan is the object aimed at. There is, however, another political motive which may be animating our Government, and that is the relief of Italy from some of the difficulties in which she is involved through her attempts at conquest in Abyssinia; and in fact both Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. CURZON admit that it is expected the expedition will divert some of the forces of the Mandi from the projected attack upon the much-harassed Italians. Mr. CURZON in alluding to the Jtalians described them as "our staunch allies," and naturally it is now asked what is meant by this phrase. Has this country entered into an alliance with the Italians and through them with the Triple Alliance? If • that be so this unexpected movement of troops on the Nile may lead to some startling developments on the European continent. Under these circum- stances it is satisfactory to hear that the Liberal leaders in the House of Commons are determined to insist upon more light being thrown upon the Government's foreign policy. Lord SALIKSURY'S methods have not hitherto been so successful that the country can afford to leave hire an entirely free hand.

AN article which appears in Blackwood 's Magazine " for SE?uhmooelbuory. „ this month would almost lead one to believe that in these days the humorists—that is, the unconscious humorists—are all in the ele- mentary schoo's. At all events the examination papers of the rising generation are an inexhaus- tible source of amusement. The schoolboy seems to be able to evolve quaint conceits not only from the lighter subjects with which he is invited to deal, but even from the dryest of dry sciences. One youthful economist, for instance, in dealing with the question of penny dinners for school children declared that "Each child receives a good deal more than a pennyworth, but the loss is not great when a great many children buy." A budding sanitarian declares that "The water communica- tion should be stopped, as water is the greatest carrier of the germs of typhoid fever." One is almost disposed to think that certain rural sanitary authorities are sarcastically alluded o in the statement that " the water that is used is carefully analysed, and wh en anything is found likely to cause dis ease, it is entirely disregarded." "Car bonny cassia " is a new way of spelling carbonic acid, and the writer of " Lack tail ducks" evidently knew more about aquatic fowl that he knew about the "lacteal ducts." Certain youthful hygienists have solemnly propounded the following maxims ; "Every house not yet infected should be disin- fected ;" "The body is covered with little holes ;" "For tea she might get a little cocoa " The nurse should not mix with anyone except the doctor ;" "One breath of morning air is worth a dozen of moonlight." There is a lack of hi storical accuracy in the assertions that " John Weslgy joined the navy in 1779, and by degrees rose to be Duke of'Wellington," and that " Pym was a cora- .. parnon of Hampden in the ship called the Pilgrim Fathers which sailed to America in 1620." We perceive greater confusion still over the Inames of historical celebrities in reading that "Sir THOMAS More lived in the reign of WILLIAM; he was a great poet; some of his poems were Ccelebs in search of a wife," Ye am' The Descent of Man.' Mariners of England,' He was also one of the greatest preachers of his time." Even sacred narrative is misapprehended occasionally. "Why was there no room in the inn ?" asked a teacher who had been rehearsing the story of the Nativity at Bethlehem. "Because it was pay day" promptly replied a little fellow belonging to a mining village where the miners were paid fortnightly or monthly.

TEA AND CONCERT IN THE PRIMITIVE METHODIST SCHOOLROOM.

With the object of raising funds towards the circuit fund, a tea meeting and concert were held in the Primi- tive Methodist schoolroom,on Wednesday evening. The following ladies presided at the tables :—Mesdames Upton, A. Davies, J. Stubbs, R. Stubbs and Collier,and Misses Hoxworth, Sayle and Myles. The after proceed- ings were presided over by Mr. William Upton. The programme was then proceeded with. Mr. A. Shingler received an encore for his singing of "The Yeoman's Wedding," and a similar compliment was paid to Miss Terry.of Frodsharn, who sang "The Holy City." Mr. Greenwood,who it may be stated, won the cornet solo competition at the Widnes Eisteddfod. performed a cornet solo, and he was succeeded by Miss Bradshaw; who sang "The Gift." The son. " Mary Lee" was in- terpreted by Mr. Parry, and Mr. song Aston, of Prod- sham, received an encore for his singing of " Rocked in the Cradle of the Deep." He responded with " The Wild Winds Blow" Messrs. Shingler and Parry contributed a duet. and Miss navies followed with " Home Deane Home." A humorious recitation was given by Mr. G. Aston, and an interesting item was an action song en- titled " The Cricket Club," which was given by the junior members of the Beehive Sewing Class. " True till Death " was next sung by Mr. J. Stubbs, and Miss Wilson was loudly applauded for her singing of "The Star of Bethlehem. A second cornet solo was rendered by Mr. Greenwood. He per- formed the test piece at the Widnes Eisteddfod,and was encored. The two concluding items were a song by Mr. F. Shingl•r,and an action song by the senior members of the Beehive Sewing Class. The duties of accompanist were satisfactorily fulfilled by Mr. W. Grounds.

13,IINCORN AND THE SHIP CANAL

A CONFERENCE AT RUNCORN.

On Thursday afternoon, a conference took place at Runcorn between representatives of the Manchester Ship Canal Company and members and officials of the Run- corn Council. On behalf of the company there were present—Mr. Bythell (chairman of the company), Mr. Collier (Bridgewater Canals Undertaking), Mr. Meadow- croft (Runcorn agent), Mr. F. Wiswall, C.E. (engineer of the Bridgewater Undertaking), and Mr. Taylor (auditor on behalf of the Manchester Corporation. The deputa- tion from the Runcorn Council consisted of Messrs. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., S. Taylor, J.P., C.C., E. Imison, J.P., J. W. Lightburn, J. Pritchard, D. Bisbrown, G. H. Kirkham, J. Jones, F. A. Lake (law clerk), and J. Wild- ing (surveyor). The first meeting place was near the recently finished slipways at the Old Quay. The representatives of the Council had several complaints to make with regard to these slipways for the repair of vessels. In the first place it was contended that the depth of the water was insufficient to admit of large vessels reaching the slip- ways at the normal height of the tide. They also pointed out that the charges for the use of blccks upon which to rest the vessel during repair,were excessive to a degree, and were a hindrance to the free use of the slipways. Then again,with regard to the small graving dock in the Old Quay yard, it was stated that the shipwrights who made use of it were obliged to bring with them their own appliances. This necessitated loss of time, and added considerably to the cost of the work in hand. It was suggested to the repre.enta- Lives of the company that both the slipways and graving dock should be efficiently equipped with all necessary appliances, and that a slight charge should be made for the use of such. After Leiring the complaints and suggestions, Mr. Bythell said, with regard to the depth of the water at the slipways, it was at least three feet deeper than the one which had existed there previously. This statement the Runcorn deputa- tion were not in a position to dense Proceeding to deal with the other complaints, Mr. Bvthell promised that a ' reduction should be made in the liarges for the appli- ances. and it was agreed that Mr. Collier should meet ' Mr. Bisbrown and Mr.tKirkham within a few days hence and arrange a scale oficbarges which should be satis- f ,ctory. Proceeding to the salt tips, the deputation con- tended that the tip was too short for the loading of vessels with broad beams, and that the material would be deposited on the ship's deck instead of in the hold. Mr. Bythell said he had seen the tip at work that day, and he thoeght it would be found very suitable for the work. With regard to the length of it, he was afraid that if it were extended it would interfere with some of the vessels being tied up. He believed the Salt Union Limited :were making arrangements for in- creasing their output from the neighbourhood, and they were providing more storage accemmodation. The Ship Canal Company were anxious to encourage the trade, and he was sanguine enough to believe that a large quantity of coal as well as salt would be shipped from the neighbourhood. The subject of dues and tolls charged for vessels using the Runcorn layby was broached tut the representatives of the company declined to speak on the subject on that occasion, but it is expected that a further conference will be held to consider the matter.

This Board met in the Boardroom at Dutton Work- house on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. J. Knowles occupied the chair, and there were also present in ad- dition to the members of the Rural Council—the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, the Rev. J. S. Simpson, Messrs.S. Yarwood,A. R. Norman, and T. Lea; Mr. J. Achton (clerk), Mr. G. Ashton (deputy clerk), and Mr. Cunnah (workhouse master).

UNTAXED FOREIGN MANUFACTURES

The Whitehaven Union transmitted copy of a petition which they had caused to be presented to the House of Commons, showing the desirability of the Legislature forthwith considering the effect of the present fiscal system of this country and foreign commercial policy, with the view of preventing the unrestricted flooding of British markets by untaxed foreign manufactures from such foreign countries as restrict (or impose tariffs upon) British exports, by which present sys- tem it is believed employment is rendered diffi- cult of attainment, destitution is increased, and trade, labour, and agriculture are prejudicially affected in this country—Mr. Lea proposed that the communica- tion be allowed to lie on the table. This was agreed to. —Mr. Heywood expressed the opinion that the subject deserved more consideration than bad been given to it, and he gave notice that he would introduce the matter at the next meeting.—The Clerk suggested that it be re- ferred to the Parliamentary Committee.—Mr.lieywood : Yes, anyway so that the subject is dealt with. It most vitally affected all Boards of Guardians.—Mr. Lewis : I thought it wanted consideration, but I was afraid that some members would call it protection.—Mr. Lea : I thought this matter was sr-Wed.—The Chairman : It is settled for to-day.—Mr. Heyw, od : It can lie on the table, and be brought up in a fortnight.

THE PROPOSED EXTENSION OF THE DINING ROOM. Mr. Samuel Davies, architect and surveyor, Runcorn. submitted a plan, specification and estimate for the pro- posed extension of the dining room at the Workhouse. According to the plan, he calculated that twenty per- sons could dine with comfort at any onetime, leaving sufficient space for atrendants.—lt was decided that the matter be referred to the Dinin, Room Committee.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY, MARCH 21st, 1696.

THE country has been both startled Another Sensation and mystified by the announcement . that another expedition is to be sent into the Soudan from Egypt.- What is the explanation of this remarkable development of the Government's miscalled polky of "repose ?" That is a question which has been asked in both Houses of Farliament,but it has not received any complete or satisfactory reply. Such answers as have been , given by Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. CURZON have in- creased rather than lessened the anxiety felt as to the objects aimed at.. We are told that the reasons tor this advance up the Nile are partly military and partly political. It is stiongly suspected that the real reasons are much more of a political than of a military nature. In fact so far as military opinions go the weight of authority seems to be decidedly adverse to the expedition. It is ad- mated that, for defensive purposes, the present Egyptian frontier at Wady Haifa is about the strongest that could be selected.So far at all events, the bravest and most fanatical followers of the 31Aum have found it impregnable, as they have been disastrously beaten whenever they have made an attempt upon it. The reason for this is that any force advancing from the South must of necessity traverse an immense waterless desert before it can come within striking distance of Wady Haifa, and wken it does emerge from these sandy plains it is almost certain to be so weakened that it is utterly unable to cope with the deend- ing force whose rescurces arc unimpaired. This was prc,ved in 1889 when NEJUMI, one of the most gallant and intrepid of the MAUDI'S emirs, led a troop of 3,300 fighting men against the Egyptian frontier. These men when they set out on their arduous journey were full of vigour and enthu- siasm, but their line of advance was strewed with dead bodies, and when they appeared on the northern margin of the desert they were described by General GRENFELL as a "poor,weakly crowd,dy- ing of hunger and thirst." In the encounter which followed NEJUMI'S army was utterly routed by the Egyptians under British guidance with a loss

At Runcorn Police Court, on Friday morning, before Mr. E. unison, au elderly man named Mark Smith, a tramp, was charged with stealing a pair of sea boots and an evercoat, from the shop ot Arnold Unsworth, Percival-lane, on the 11th inst. It was stated that the goods were seen safe at ten o'clock on Wednesday night, and that they were missed half an hour later. The ac- cused was apprehended at Frodsham on the following day with the articles in his possession. He was handed over to P.C. Gregory, and brought to Runcorn. When charged with the offence he said he had purchased them from another man.—Superintendent Okell asked for a remand until the following Friday, in order to give him an opportunity of ascertaining the previous history cf the prisoner.—The application was grantel IRUNCORN AND WIDNES LIBERALS AT BILLIARDS., The return billiard match between the members of the Runcorn and Widnes Liberal Clubs was played off at Widnes on Wednesday night. 1,383 RUNCORN FEHR LIBRARY.

_ A meeting of the Runcorn Free Library Committe-‘ was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Hall, on Tues- day evening, under the presidency of Dir. William Handley. There were also present :—Rev. J. S. Simpson, Mr. W. J. Cunningham, Mr. T. Bfley and Mr. J. D. Jones (librarian). Accounts amounting to £59 17s. 6d. were examined and passed for payment, £36 of which was for rent of ptemises. The following gifts were acknowledged :—From the trustees of the British Museum, supplementary matter for fac-similes of Royal Historical and Literary autographs; by the Federated Institution of Miners' Engineers, Newcastle-on-Tyne, report of proceedings of conference on Inland Navigation and J. H. Merivale's paper on "The Education of Min- ing Engineers" ; from Messrs. T. B. Browne, Limited, ," Press Directory, 1896 " ; Lady Meux "Life of Alexan- der the Great" and free library reports from Clapham, Hove and Widnes. The librarian's report for the month of February showed that 2,308 books were issued, being an increase of 220 when compared with the correspond- ing month of last year. The issues were in the following departments as follows :—'l heology 48, philosophy 8. science 124, history, &c., 171, poetry 12, fiction 1,700, miscellaneous 217, music 33.—The report was adopted.

FRODSHAK

FRODSHAM LORDSHIP PARISH COUNCIL.--A meeting of the Parish Council of Frodsham Lerdship was held on Menday evening under the presidency of Mr. Charles Reynolds. A discussion arose as to the ventilation of the sewer at Overton, and it was recommended that the manhole be blocked up and that a ventilating shaft be constructed and attached to a tree on Mr. Edmund's property, as pointed out to the Inspector of Nuisances, on the usual terms, and that the matter be referred to the Joint Committee of Frodsham and Frodsham Lord- ship.—Mr. Lewis moved the confirmation of the minutes at the meet;ng of the Rural Council on Tues. day.—Mr.Heywood said he protested against the recom- mendations being confirmed so far as they related to the closing of the manhole. It belonged to Frodshain and should not be blocked up.—Mr. Williams said they had spent £l7 or £lB to improve it, and if it was now to be blocked up, and other expenses incurred, Frodshain Township would have something to say about it.—Mr. Lewis said that the Lordship would pay the expenses they incurred.—Mr. Williams said the recommendation did not say so.—The minutes were confirmed subject to the matter relative to the manhole, being referred to the joint committee.

- WESLEYAN YOUNG PEOPLE'S SALE OF WORE.—MYS. E. J. Guest, of Elelsby, opened a .small sale of work pro- moted by the young people gn connection with the Trinity Wesleyan Church, on Wednesday afternoon. The sale was held in Trinity Lecture Hall and was well at- tended. The stallholders were: fancy stall, Mrs. Renton and Miss Ken ; refreshment stall, Miss Wrench, Miss Booth, Miss Rimmer and Miss L. Edwards; flower stall, Miss E. Kent and Miss Carter, assisted by a number of little girls. A three-fold object was in view, viz., that of raising funds for the Zenana missions,Dr. Stevenson's Home, and the Rev. Charles Garrett's workpn Liverpool. —Mr. Guest in declaring the sale of work open referred to the success of the Zenana missions. The Rev. It. Renton also addressed the assembly. At night a num- ber of entertainments were given. SUICIuII OF AN OSTLEEL-0a Sionday morning, at the Bear's Paw Hotel, Frodsham, Mr. A. C. Churton held AR inquiry into the circumstances connected with the death of Thomas Flavin, aged 36 years, ostler, who commi.ted suicide by taking prussic acid, on S tturday morning.— George ilassall, ostler at the Bear's Paw Hotel, deposed that on Saturday morning he went into the stable,where he was joined by Plevin, who said he was going to make himself away, and when asked the reason why, he rt- plied that did not matter. He then bade witness "good- bye." Deceased went a few yards from the stable door, pulled a bottle out of his left hand trousers pocket simi- lar to the one produced, and drank the contents. Wit- ness rushed into the house to tell the inmates, and returned immediately, when he found deceased on his back in the loose box, with the bottle in his hand. A messenger was despatched for Dr. Selby, and that gentle- man, who was soon in attendance, pronounced life ex- tinct.—John Maurice Jones, chemist and druggist, Church-st., stated that shortly after 8.30 on Saturday morning, deceased came to his shop and asked for some prussic acid to poison a dog, saying it was for Alec Bate, who was a customer of his.—William Youd, landlord ot the Bear's Paw Hotel, in answer to the Coroner, who wished to knew if any reason could be assigned for the act, stated that through an accident when Flavin was driving he had had to pay £l4O, and had consequently warned him that if anything of the sort occurred again he would be obliged to discharge him. Deceased had been in his employ eight years.—The jury recorded a verdict of "Committed suicide whilst in a state of tem- porary insanity." OPENING OF A NEW ORGAN.—The new organ in con- nection with the Primitive Methodist Sunday School was opened on Sunday afternoon by Mr. William Cooke, of Runcorn, who played selections from the works of Gull- mont, Bach, Mozart. Jude and Tellam which displayed the qualtities of the instrument. Mr. You'd, of Fred- sham, contributed a violin solso. Several vocal selec- t' s were also given. Miss Wilson of Runcorn sang two soks in a finished manner, and Mr. Wesley C. Cooke,also of Runcorn, sang with much effect. "Honour and Arms," and "Remember the Sabbath day to keep it holy," were the two solos sung by Mr. Atherton. The latter solo had been specially composed by Mr. Cooke, and Mr. Atherton din full justice to it. The choir also sang an anthem. The Rev. T. Bransall, conducted the service in the abscence of Mr. John Wilson, of Runcorn. Mr. William Cooke again presided at the organ at the evening service. Mr. Atherton gave a fine rendering of "Nazareth "and Mr. Wesley C. Cooke sang " The Holy City," and " Ora Pro Notes "with great expression.

NOTES ON G.A.M.Es AND ATHLETICS. RUGBY FOOTB&LL.

The members of the Runcorn team bad the privilege of enjoying a much needed rest on Saturday through the action of the Rochdale Hornets' club. As stated in our previous issue, the Runcorn team strongly objected to journeying to Heywood, when the Rochdale duo had shown such partiality as to be willing to travel to both Oldham and Manninghain. The reason for their doing so ia that they are to be treated generously with the gate money, which will far exceed that which would have been taken on the Runcorn ground. But what is good for the goose is good tor the gander,and considering the importance of a victory to either of the three clubs in their present position, the Rochdale team might just as well have run the gauntlet of coming to Runcorn. But they have thought otherwise, and a week-day match will be the result at an early date. Many Runcornians took the opportunity of the vacant fixture to journey to Warrington to see the champion Manningham team meet the wirepuller, and they were just a little disap- pointed to see the latter lose, for notning would have given them greater pleasure than to have seen the colours of the Yorkshire team lowered.

For the match on Saturday at Runcorn, when the home team will meet Bradford, several alterations in the composition of the Runcorn fifteen have ben made. The team hag been selected as follows Houghton, back; T. Warder, G. Robinson, J. Butterworth and H. Myers, three-Quarter backs; J. Faulkner and J. Nickson, half-backs; W. Faulkner, H. Farmer, R. Reece, S. Walker, G. Moores, 0. Hughes, J. Langley and W. Lightfoot.

ST. HELENS THEATRE ROYAL

PRODUCTION OF A NEW PLAY.

A new romantic play entitled "The Roll of the Drum," by the eminent. novelist, Mr. Joseph Hatton, will be produced for the first time on any stage at the Theatre Royal, St. Ilelens, on Monday night next. The new play is. dramatised by the author himself from his famous book "When Greek meets Greek," which first appeared in the columns of "The People," and sine has had a tremendous sale in book form. The company engaged to create the parts in the new play is an ex- ceedingly streng one,and consists entirely of well-known Loudon artistes. The list is headed by Mr. Murray Carson,who assumes a dual rcle. He has appeared lately at the Garrick, the Adelphi, and Terry's Theatre, and shortly intends to play " The Roll of the Drum," in the metropolis. Then there is Miss Bessie Hatton, the talented daughter of the author, who has been quite lately at the Garrick, with Mr. Willard in "The Professor's Love Story," in which she made a great success as Lucy White. Miss Louise Moodie is also an extremely well known actress and Miss Essex Dane has been pronounced as one of London's most accomplished artistes. Mr. Percival, of the Lyric Theatre, London, and Mr_. Loring Ferine are also included in the cast. Tha music is by Mr. Sidney Jones, the well-known corn- poQer who is now with Mr. Wilson Barrett in " The Sign of the Cross." The costumes are by Mr. W. Clark- son, costumier to the Queen, and the scenery by Mr. Walter Johnson, of Drury-lane Theatre are everything that could be desired irom an artistic point of view. It s not often locally that we have the opportunity of u dging a play in its first production and it will be quite n iuteresting evening for the St. Helens public.

MAKE LIFE ENJOYABLE.

Loss of health means loss of all which makes life en- joyable ; for, in addition to the bodily pain and suffering which it cause, it entails a vast amount of mental pain and anxiety, especially in the homes of the working classes. It brings in its train a host of discomforts which retard the recovery of the sufferer, and hinder, instead of assisting, Nature to perform her functions. When a working man suffers from any ailment which prevents his following his daily avocation, he is natur- ally filled with anxiety regarding the maintenance of those depending on him for their daily sustenance. His very love for his family retards and hinders his speedy recovery, and his spirits become depresied at the antici- pation of the gloomy prospects before them in the event of a long continuance of his illness. It is well known that depression of spirits often proves a serious bar to a speedy recovery.

These results arise in addition to the various incoil- veniences and discomforts which the sufferer and his fami'y experience as direct results of his illness, such as additional expenses for luxuries for the sick, with in- creased difficulty to obtain those luxuries, or even the necessities of life.

Mestof the various forms of disease which trouble the labouring classes of the country, and which are often attributable, more or less, to some unhealtbly condition of their daily work, may be classei under a few leading heads.

To avoid thg, discomforts arising from various forms of diseasc,every working man should scok the aid of THE VEGETABLE TONIC,

Which has gained a world-wide renown as the most wonderful medicinal discovery of the age; and as un- felling remedy it is prescribed by many leading physi- cians in various rilments. For Bronchitis, Asthma, Coughs, and Colds. Shortness of Breath, Neuralgia, General Weakness, Low Spirits, Nervousness, Indigestion, Influenza.

SUDDEN DEA.III OF A CHILD AT THE WORKHOUSE.

CORONER'S INQUIRY.

Mr. S. Brighouse held at inquest at the Warring- ton Workhouse on Thursday morning, on the body of Lily Norman, infant daughter of Ralph Norman, outdoor porter, 21. School-brow. The child died on ISunday under circumstances which the police considered justified a Coroner's inquiry. Inspector M'Ateer watched the proceedings on behalf of the National Society for the 'Prevention of Cruelty to Children.—Previous to calling evidence, the Coroner said that the case was reported to him because the police thought there might have been some neglect on the part of the parents in connection with the death of the child, and in consequence of that, he had ordered a post-mortem examination to be made, and on their part the police had made all the inquiries they could. He understood that the result of the post- mortem examination did not show that death had resulted from neglect, and, therefore, he did not think the in- quiry would be very long.—Elizabeth Norman, the mother of the child, and at present an inmate of the Workhouse,was then called, and said that her house was not broken up. Her child was six months old, and had been a very healthy child until a fortnight before, and she and it were admitted into the Workhouse. At that time it had a fit,and Dr. Langdale, ot the Infirmary,haxl seen it twice, On Thursday last week, her husband beat her and turned her out of the house and she had to find shelter for the night at a house in Gandy's-yard, off Tillev-st. The people at this house could not be troubled with the crying of the child, and the next day she went to Mr. Aukland, relieving officer, and got an order for the Workhouse, where she had been since. She thought that her husband was still keeping the house going. The baby died at two o'clock on Sunday afternoon. She had done all she could for it. It was the only child she had. She had been married 15 months. Dr. Braide, visiting surgeon of the workhouge, stated that he first saw the deceased child on March 14. It was fairly well nourished. It died rather suddenly on Sun- day afternoon. He made a post mortem examination, and found it a fully developed child. The internal organs were all healthy with the exception of the brain and membranes of the brain, which showed distinct signs of congestien and inflammatory changes in the membranes. It suffered from meningitis, which was undoubtedly the cause of death. There was nothing which would lead him to suspect neglect of the child.— Inspector Cox informed the jury that Dr. Langdale had told him that he had attended the child for bronchitis, but had said nothing about fits.—The Coroner said that he did not think the jury need trouble themselves any further, as it seemed to him that nothing had been done to the child which was open to question.—After a short consultation, the jury returned a verdict that the de- ceased died from natural causes, viz., meningitis as shown by the medical evidence. They asked the Coroner whether the husband, who was in an anteroom. should be called in so that he could be cautioned about his behaviour to his wife, but the Coroner infor.ned them that he could not, as there was nothing to show that the child's death was due to his conduct.

WARRINGTON v. MANNINGFIAM.

The visit of the fmous Yorkshire team which at pre- sent bead the competition in the Northern League caused great interest in Warrington on Saturday, and tbe largest gate ot the present seas m assembled to witness what proved to be a most interesting and enjoy- able match. A gate of over 9,000 was present,and these composed about 300 Manninehamites, besides large compataies from Widnes and Runcorn, at which places there was no match. The space which is reserved for spectators presented a fine sight, for every point ot vantage was ocenpied, and the pressure on the boards which enclose the field was so great that a portion gave way during the progress of the match, but fortunately, no one was injured, and the game was also not inter- fered with. The game was not commenced with quite as great punctuality as the more recent matches have teen, owing to the late arrival of Brown, the Yorkshire county threequarter, and about ten minutes late, Manningham entered the field with only 14 men. The following was the Warrington team :—Heesom, full back ; Carey, Barber, Cross, and Lawless. three- quarters; Bate and Fligeinbottoia, half backs ; Taylor. Dakin,•Hill, Saunders, Wilcock, Fairhurst, Burrows,and Andrews, forwards. _

It will thus be seen that Nevins and Swift were absent trom the advertised team. Mr.T. Melleder,of Rochdale; was the referee, and some of his decisions did not find much favour with the spectators. Barber won the toss. and decided to take advantage of a slight breeze which blew towards the pavilion goal. WIDNES v. BRIGHOUSE

Brighouse Rangers somewhat easily accounted for Widnes at Lane Head, Brighouse, on Saturday. There was only a very moderate attendance. Both teams turned up in full force, and the few spectators r,rwrit had the pleaaure of witnessing a fairly interesting game. On the Bangers' side a new full back was tried in the person of one Tom Thomas. The game started in a ding (long fashion,and before the Rang*rs could he fairly dis- lodged from a position which they took tin immediately at the commencement,Widnes had a penalty goal scored against them from the foot of Fred Wood. Waddington added an unconverte(l try, and at half-time Brighouse led by six points to mine. On restarting. Risna,n almost dropped a goal with areallv clever shot,and Nevitt twice ran to within measurable distance of the Brighouse line, Thomas bringing him down each time. Towards the close of the game the Ra.nlers overpowered their oppon• ents,and as a result of some smart play by Waddington, Croft scored close to the posts, Hartley converting. Widnes strove desperately hard to chalk a point ; but their efforts were in vain,and Brighouse rata out winners by one goal, one penalty goal, and one try al points) to Widnes nothing. The score just about represents the winners' superiority. Forward, Widnes played strongly, and their backs passed prettily, hut they lacked finish. Brennan played excellently at full hack; H. Peters and Nevitt were the best of the three-quarters, whilst at half Drummond made several smart rims. The hest of the losing forwards were A. Peters, Johnson. and Whelan. On the home side, the new back made a verr creditable debut. Ross,Hartley, and Croft were conspicuous be- hind,and in the front rank, Nichol, Pullman, and Wood were the best of a very clever set.

SALVATION ARMY INDUSTRIES.

A report issued on Wednesday from the social head. quarters of the Salvation Army shows that the cost of supporting an able-bodied man in one of the Army's int &Istria' estab.ishments called the "elevators "amounted to 24. weekly in exc..ss of his earnings. At the Hadleigh farm colony h kery department 47,000 loaves were made and sold in 12 months, in addition to upwards of four tons of cake,tvvo huneredweight of biscuits, and 148,000 I-Alf-penny cakes. The farm and dairy sections at Had- leic& have under control over 681 acres. There are now on the farm 500 pigs. 470 sheep and lambs, 150 cows, 15 horses, and 200 poultry. The value of the produce of the farm was £20,800, and the income from fruit alone was £1,500. During the past week 200 tons of sorted paper was sold in connection with the salvage section -of the City Col my. The waste paper and rag industry provides daily employment for over 500 men, and the match factory. founded on anti-sweating and wholesale lines, continues to prosper. The girls in the 'knitting factory have during the past three months made I £4OO worth of goods.

LANCASHIRE AND CHESMRE ASSO. CIATION OF BAPTIST CHURCHES.

===== 0002601_18960321_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
SPRING SESSION.

On Tuesday the spring session of the Western district of the Lancashire and Cheshire Association of Baptist Churches was held at Hill Cliffa Church, near Warring- ton. At 11 a.m.there was a meeting for ministers only when there were present delegates from 38 churches,and the proeee-linevs were presided over by the Rev. Sidney W. Bowser, B.A. Devotions were led by the Rev. J. Kemp, of Wigan, and Mr. Kenworthy. of Warrington. A very interesting paper on "Hill Cliffe and its Heroes" was read by the pastor of the church, the Rev. J. S. Hughes. Several of those present discussed the subject matter of the paper. Dinner was provided, and at 2 p.m. there was a committee meeting, when cases of mission grants were considered and various other items of public business transacted. Following this there was a pablic service, when the preacher, the Rev. J. Thomas, M.A. (Myrtle-st., Liverpool), successor to the Rev. H. S. Browne, delivered an excellent and characteristic dis- course from Romans Bth,verses 36 and 39. Tea was pro- vided,and the wants of the visitors attended to by Mrs. Hughes, Mrs. Bradford, Mrs. Booth. Mrs. Ansdale, Mrs. Kenworthy. Miss Rowson,Miss Bradford, Miss Edwards, Miss Worrell, Mrs. Grounds, and Miss Kenworthy. The principal occurrence -au the meeting in the even- ing when the chair was occupied by Mr. R. Pedley (ex- mayor of Crewe), and the subject of speech was "Free Church Principles." With the Chairman were the Revs. J. Aldis (Little Leigh), Alex Gordon, M. A. (Egremont), and Charles Green, of Liverpool. The CHAIRMAN said he had had for many years a great interest in Hill Cliffe Church, though it was no 53 years since he first visited the place. He was then on a visit to their late friend, Mr. Swinton, who accompanied him to the Sunday school anniversary. Since then he had felt a great interest in the chapel and its many as- sociations. At home he was associated . ith a eaurch somewhat similarly situated—that was,in a very retired spot—and in connection with which he had just cele- brated his jubilee. (Applause.) In 1844 he was bap- tised in a large pond of water, and he thought that it then required more courage to face baptism than to-day. Referring to the subject cf discourse he said he did not think there ever was a period in the history of tkeir church in this country when it was mere important that they should all thoroughly understand the principles upon which their church life was based. (Bear, hear.) They had the privilege of choosing their own creed and selecting their own ministers,end they had refused State aid and were not bound by the golden fetters which the members of the Episcopalian Church were to-day. Their free church principles were very much better and wider than that. They believed that they were the result of the divine teaching of the great founder of their church, Jesus Christ. (Hear, hear.) Referring to the National Free Church Congress in Nottingham held the other day Mr. Pedley said he was astonished at the statement made by Mr. Hughes that the Methodists, Baptists, and Con- giegationalists together had within the last 150 years provided equal religious accommodation to the Estab- lished Church. (Applause.) Mr. Pedley concluded by expressing confidence in free church principles. The Rev. JouN ALMS, in the course of a lenethy and capable address, said that free church principl-s were Absolute loyalty to Jesus Christ ; freedom from church patronage and control ; purity of communion ; and absolute autonomy—right of self-government in Christ. In conclusion he asserted that the Free Church had, not- withstanding their faults, been the greatest blessing to the land. (Applause.) Addresses, each dealing with a different aspect of the subject were delivered by the Rev. A. Gordon and the Rev. C. Green.

A satisfactory collection was taken and the proceeri- ings closed with the singing of the doxology and the pronouncing of the benediction.

WARRINGTON EXTENSION BILL.

THE EXTENSION GRANTED.

A Local Government Provisional Order Bill for an ex- tension of the borough boundaries of Warrington, on Weanesday came before a committee of the Rouse of Commons, of which Sir James Kitson is chairman. The object is to confirm a Local Goverment Provisional Order so altering the limits of the borough as to include the following additional areas :—Part of the townships cf Appletoo with Hull, Latchford Without,Walton Infer- ior, Little Sankey, and Poulton-with-Fearnhead. Mr. Pembroke Stephens, Q.C., Mr. Balfour Browne, Q.C.,and Mr. Sutton represented the promoters. The Lmdon and North Western Railway ,_oinpany petitioned, their coun- sel being Mr. Pope, Q.C., Mr. Littler, Q.C., and Mr. Rickards.

At the outset, Sir J Kitson called Mr. Pembroke Step- hens's attention to the fact that Mr. Moon, a member of the committee, was a shareholder in the London and North-western, and that at the time the committee was selected the honourable member was not aware that the London and North-western were opposing. Mr. Pembroke Stephens expressed his entire confidence in the tribunal as at present constituted, and added that ineinLers having private interests sometimes were led to lean a I;ttle to the other side.

Mr. Rickards: We are obviously satisfied, and do not believe that the fact of Mr. Moon holding shares in the company will affect his mind one way or the other. Mr. Pembroke Stephens then proceeded with his open- ing for the promoters. The Bill, he said, was the result of a local inquiry which the Department had made, and it would not merely settle difficulties between the borough and the immediately adjacent districts, but be- tween the two counties; and the two counties were quite in accord. The boundary would keep to the dry land. The northern bank of the Manchester Ship Canal would be in o e county; the canal itself and the southern bank in the other county. By arrangement under this order neighbouring authorities were satisfied, and had actually petitioned in favour, and although county councils did not,as a matter of practice,formally petition,evideuce of consent could be,if nectssary, given in the present case. There was really no discordant note sounded,except by the London and North-Western Rail- way Company. in 1330 there was a Bill promoted for various purposes by Warrington, including the transfer of the waterworks and a certain exten- sion, but the outside authorities were not then approached, and the storm usually raised by proposals of annexation was brought about, and the scheme met with considerable opposition. But there WAS this to be said, that at the time stated the canal was still in process of construction, and it was -then stated that the promoters were trying to take as their boundary the line of the canal, which might be altered within the limits of the deviation. Warrington, of late, had felt the pressure of the joint committee which had charge of the purification of rivers, and it would shortly become its tt, rn to get into line. Warrington had already received certain formal notices, and had to look forward at no distant date to a rearrangement of sewage matters. That was one point, and the necessity had been prec.- nitated by another thing, namely, the works of the Manchester Ship Canal themselves. Another fact to be taken into consideration was the increase of population in the townships proposed to be included, and, further, there was a nectsiity for improved water supply. He did not know how long the London and North-Western were going to oppose the wishes of everybody in the dis- trict, but if they persisted in their opposition he must tell the committee that the proposed boundary had been recommended in the strongest way by various authori- ties, and by none more so than ty the county itself. A differential- scale of rating was asked for over periods of five, ten, and 15 years, ana that was the general line of the arrangement. The petitioning company said in their petition that they would not get any benefit from the increase of the town; but it was difficult to see how a scheme such as this could be car- ried out without their receiving a corresponding advan- tage. Again, the company asserted there was "no pub- lic ground." His reply was that everybody wished it. The proposals of 1390 might have been premature, but in 1896 the conditions were changed, and were as diffe.- ent as light from dark. There was reason to believe that under the extension the rates over the larger area woald be less.

The Chairman: That is not our general experience. Mr. Pembroke Stephens: I can only tell you, Sir James, that calculations have been made, and that the effect if the differential rates upon the London and North-Western Railway will not exceed a maximum at the end of the period of £l5O. They will pay exactly the same proportion as everybody else. Mr. Thomas Longdin, borough engineer and surveyor of Warrington, was then called, and examined by -Mr. Sutton in support of the Bill. Mr. Longdin generally bore out the statement of promoters' counsel. There was proposed for inclusion in the new borough bound- ary, for which no local authority would accept respon- sibility. and which was neither lighted nor policed. lie pointed out the great objections to a continuance of the prnant system of sewage outfall. The prevailing smells were abominable, and certain houses had no tenants in consequenre. In 1890 there were no such depositing of filth on the Mersey bank as now existed.

The Chairman : Is it necessary tl go fully into this ? Mr. Sutton: If the amalgamation is carried out, the sewage scheme becomes an easy one, but I do not wish to ask questions other than such as will assist the com- mittee.

—Mr.-Longdin then, replying to a general question of Mr. Sutton,sald if they, took in thq outlying district's the only place where they could put their sewage works was Little Sankey. At the close of tha examination in chief of Mr. Long - din, Mr. Sutton called attention to the absence of coun- sel on the other side to cross-3xamine, and consequently Proposed to call the clerk of the Lancashire County Council.

Mr. F. C. Hutton, clerk of the peace for Llncashire, and clerk of the Lancashire County Council,was accord- ingly examined by Mr. Sutton. Mr. Hutton said he at- tended the inquiry at Warrington on April 2nd,1895,and afterwards he consulted with the town clerk of Warring- ton and the clerk of the Cheshire County Council. They agreed on certain conditions intended to safegua the interests of both counties,and these proposals were agreed to by the parliamentary committee on April 19. The question of making the Ship Canal the boundary was fully considered.

Mr. Longdin, recalled for cross-examination by Mr. Rickards on hehalf of the railway company, said he gave evidence in 1890, when the centre line of the waterway of the Ship Canal was proposed as a bo,mdary. Be did not think th waterway was in the same way suggested when the application for this provisional order was deposited in 1894.. With regard to population in the several townships now proposed te be included, houses had been built and works extended since the promoters were before Parliament in 1890.

The next witness for the promoters was Mr. William Owen, architect and surveyor, of Warrington, valuer to the Warrineton Union, and surveyor to the district c,uncil. He reearded the proposed boundary of the boroueh as not only desirable, but necessary for the development of the ~everal townships proposed to be included.—Mr. Littler: Won't the Ship Canal continue to enjoy an exemption under the Public Health Act ? Witness : I should think they will.—Mr. Pembroke Stephens : But both the canal and the railway company will have the same principles applied to them, and can vnu see any hardship in that ?—I can see none what- ever. —Mr. Littler : That is a rash statement in answer to a leading question, because it is exactly the reverse. Mr. Robert Vaughan Matthews, civil engineer,residing at Little Sankey,examined by Mr. Sutton, said the pro- posed terms were satisfactory to the district cnuncil. Lord Lilford had offered an acre and a half of land for a recreation ground on condition that they went into the hornueh of Warrington.—Mr. Littler said he did not so interpret the letter.—The Chairman having read his lordship's communication said the letter spoke of a cer- tain contingency, but it did not affect the case one way or the other. Mr. Pembroke Stephens was about to call Mr. Man- sergh,but the Chairman intimated the committee had beard enough on the question of boundary,and some evi- dence might he called as to rating. The Referee : The Chairman suggests you should show why the L. and N.W. are to be taxed at the full within the .dded areas. Mr. Pembroke Stephens: With great respect they will have the same benefit of the differential rating as the added districts. The Referee : It does not appear that it is'so when I look at the order. Mr. J. L. Tunstall,Fellosv of the Tnstitute of Chartered Arcnuntants,and clerk to the Little Sankey Parish Coun- cil, called on the question of rating, said the tendency had been to decrease for many years. notwithstanding that a bad el..ss of property was taken in under the scheme of 1890.

On the con-ciUsion of the case for the promoters, Mr. Littler addressed the committee for the London and North-western Company. He submitted that this scheme would not benefit the company in any way whatever, and was entirely unreasonable in character, He objected airlczether to the theoretical figures of Mr. Tunswtallh, whiol he reaarded as founded purely upon fallacies. It wa most unfair to saddle the railwas company it burdens of increased taxation from which the Ship Canal Company would be exempt. He submitted a series of what he regarded as precedents against the present pro-

The petitioners did not call evidence, and the whole case being now before the committee, the room was clear-d. After a consnitation of a few moments, the committee-room was again thrown open. The Chairman t en said : I have to announce that the deeiqinn of the committee is that this order be con- firmed.

THE MAN WHO CURSED DRINK.

At the Warringtoa Borough Police Colnt on Wednes- day, before Mr. John FaiTclough (chairman), and Mr. J. Geddes, Nathan Ellis, 20, Barry-street, was charged with being dtunk and disorderly in Barry-3tr,et at 11 o'clock on Tuesday night.—He pleaded not guilty. Defendant is the man who a sl4ort time ago in giving evidence against his wife who was prosecuted for drunk- enness said in the box that there would not be a happier woman in Warrington but for than " d—' drink."

P.C. Wood stated that about 11 o'clock on Tuesday night he was called to Barry-street where he found defendant quarrelling with his wife. He was very drunk and witness tried to persuade him to be quiet. Defend- ant kicked his wife about the street, and struck her several times in his presence. He went in the house but came out again and was going to re-cpmmence his dis- turbance so witness locked him up. His wife had a black eye and several marks upon her face. She waS sobsr at the time.

Inspector Taylor said that he was in the Police Statiod when defendant was brought in and he was very drunk at the time. His wife had said that morning that defendant caused the injuries to her face. Defendant: I was not drunk but excited. She (his wife) wmil raving drunk. I have had no drink for a fortnight.

Jose-ph Flamer, a witness called by defendant br his defence, said that he had nothing to say except that defendant had had a drink or two of beer. Mr. Fairclough : And he sail he had had no drink for a fottnight.

The Chief Constable (Mr. L. Talbot) said the defen- dant had been very troublesome to the police for somo time. Once he was in the box to give evidence and used bad language t 3fore the Bench. Defendant (with sarcasm) : Is it the Chief Constable that is prosecuting. Mr. Talbot : Yes; Just the very man. (Laughter.) The defendant was fined 103. and costs or 14 days. As he was being conducted to the cells prisoner turned round and seeing his wife in court, shook his fist at her and :aid " You b when I come back bs the death of you." _

- The Cinef Constable then had him brought back and placed in the box again. Sergeant Chantler then gave formal evidence of the threat. Mrs. Eilis,whose face was in a fearful condition, came forward and said that her husband and another woman had so injured her. She did not want him sent to gaol. She admitted t Ning afraid of him. The magistrates said that in view of the threat and the present state of his wife, they had decided that de- fendant would have to find two sureties of £lO each ,to keep the peace for six mt,nths, or go to goal for a month.

Prisoner: Thank you. I'll have to go to goal then.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL

ELECTIONS.

THE NOMINATIONS. Four o'clock on Mondoy afternoon was the last hour fixed for the nomination of candidates for the election of six councillors for the Urban District Council of Run- corn. A list of the candidates are appended. It will be seen that two of the retiring councillors are returned unopposed. These are Mr. James Pritchard, for Heath ward, and Mr. John Jones, of the London and North-. Western Railway Hotel, for Newtown ward. No fewer than five candidates have been nominated for Mersey ward, the retiring representatives of which (Mr. R. H. Posnett), does not seek re-election. Of the remaining three wards, viz.:—Halton, Central, and Bridgewater, two candidates have been nominated for each, but it is very likely that there will be withdrawals as both Mr. William Handley and Mr. E. Gatley have been nomin- ated for two wards. The last day for candidates to ; withdraw is to-day (Friday), at four o'clock,when it will be definitely known who intend going to the poll. Mr. F. A. Lake, law clerk to the Council, is the returning officer. The candidates,with the names of their pro- posers and seconders are as follows HALTON WARD :—Ellis Galley, 3, John-street, trade waterman's society, proposed by James Banner, 30, Union-street, seconded by Matthew John Sweetman, 2, Malcolm-street ; Francis Wiswall, Stanley Villas, civil engineer, proposed by Simnel Taylor, Rydal Mount, seconded by John Price, Halton-road. MERSEY WARD :—Ellis Gatly, proposed by Joseph Owen, 26,801 d-street, seconded by John Taylor,4,Albert- terrace ; Charles Dutton, Wilson's Hotel, licensed victualler, proposed by Eliza Turner, 1., Irwell-lane, seconded by Edward Johnsone, 35, Bridge-street; John Baptist Gastaidi ; propc sed by John Morris, 21, Bridge-street, seconded by Charles Dutton. Wilson's Hotel ; William Handley, Heath-road, Higher Runcorn, Savings Bank Actuary, proposed by John Bate, 61, Bridge-st., seconded by Joseph Henderson, 4, Pool-lane ; Jonathan Horsefield, Cinal-at., coal merchant, proposed by Arthur Riley, 49, Bridge-street, seconded by James Wheat, 11, Bridge-street. CENTRAL WARD :—John Baptist Gastaidi, Irwell-lana clerk in holy orders, proposed by Thomas Kennedy, Church-street, seconded by Peter Phillips, I,Church-st. ; Joseph Pritchard, Halton-road, Haltoo,watchmaker,pro- posed by James Leo, 38, High-st., seconded by Richard Moores, 43, High-st. BRIDGE WATER WARD :—Murdoch McKenzie.l Clarence Terrace surgeon, proposed by George Heasley, 6, Tren- tham-se., seconded by James Howie.Hamilton,36, Leins- ter Gardens ; William Handley,proposed by John Capoer 52, Egerton-street, seconded by George Gibson, 60, Egerton-street.

NEWTOWN WARD :—John Jones, 11. Lowland-road, licensed victualler, proposed by Samuel Yarwood, 40, Albert-3treet,seconded by Richard Marsh, 3 and 5 Low- land-road ; also by Frederick Lewis, 101 Greenway- road, and Alfred Ashbrook, 76 Greenway-road. HEATH WARD :—James Pritchard, The Elms, Higher Runcorn, proposed by Philip Speakman, The Mount, sec onded by John W. Lightburn, Heath House.

SEAMEN'S INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. There was again a large attendance at the weekly entertainment provided in the Seamen's Institute on Tuesday evening. A miscellaneous programme was provided, and the proceedings were presided over by Mr. Shaw (missionary). Pianoforte solos were per- formed by Mr. Full, and Mr. G. Finney was successful in securing an encore for his performance on the mouth organ. The vocalists were Mr. J. Prire, who was encored, Mr. Harmon and Mr. G. Finney, A dialogue was also given by Mis% Lythgoe and the Misses Dean. Mr. Full ably accompanied on the piano. The usual vote of thanks concluded a pleasant evening's entertain- ment.

ASSOCIATION

MEETING AT WARIZINGTuN.

A public meeting under the auspices of the British Women's Temperance Association, Warring- ton Branch, was held at the Bold-st. Lecture Hall, last week. There was a large attendance, and the chair was occupied by Mrs. John Fairclough, who was supported on the platform by Miss Agnes Slack, hon. sec. of the World's Women's Christian Temperance Association, Miss Bessie Dutton, Mrs. H. Roberts, Mrs. Clay. and Mrs. J. Yonge. The' meeting was opened by the sin..;ing of the well- known hymn, "Onward Christian soldieis," after which Miss Dutton engaged in prayer. Mrs. FAIRCLOUGH, in her opening address, said that she must begin by making an apology as that was the first time that she had ever presided over a public meet- ing. She had to introduce to them Miss Slack. She had had the pleasure of hearing Miss Slack some time ago in Burnley, and she was delighted with the sensible and practical address she thee gave. She thought that the audience would have the same opinion after hearing her that night. It was no use denouncing the sins or the weaknesses of other people if they had no feasible remedy suggested. Miss Slack, who was the hon. secretary of the National British Women's Temper- ance Association, would no doubt be able to point out the disease and its antidote. The best thing was to try to do something. Drink was ruining the country and paralysing everything. She thought this was shown by the fact that many people scarcely liked to do anything against ittowing to the different organisations connected with it. The Dean of Norwich once said that the drink was like the Caspian Sea, that great lake in South- Eastern Europe, about which so little was known. He said that this received all the tributary streams, all the rainfall and all the snows from the mountains, but it always kept a dead level. And so the drunkard it seemed to him never did any- thing for anybody. She was inclined to think that drunkards were worse than that, as they went lower down and did not preserve a dead level. The best thing was to get hold of the children. (Applause.) If they never tasted, they would never want it. She was afraid that Warrington was a backward town in anything con- nected with temperance work. It made little headway, but she believed they were doing a little. She believed in hereditary taint. Ste did not think that alcoholic ancestry always meant alcoholic descendants, but it meant degeneration, less vitality, and diminished long- evity. She thought that more could be done by the Christian churches on behalf of total abstinence. She did not believe so much in temperance,as in total abstin- ence. (Applause.) It had been said that total abstin- ence was un-English. She did not thing so, as it some- times required courage to be a teetotaler, and English- men loved courage. It was not always plain sailing to be a teetotaler. It weuld be a good thing if everyone would do his utmost in the cause of temperance. (Ap- plause.) Miss AGNES SLACK at the commencement of her ad- dress said that she always had an "odd "way at the meetings she addressed and explained that that was at the end of her address she went round and asked every woman in the room to sign the pledge. She asked them to join the British Women's Temperance Association, as to join such an organization was to give power to them. It was wonderful what could be done by organization. She prayed that they would all help to get the souls and bodies of the drunkards free from the dreadful power of drink. There was nothing so harmful to the souls and bodies of men and women as drink. There was nothing causing so much irreligion and poverty, and so much pauperism as drink. They should vote to get the laws changed. She was determined to bother the Govern- ment as long as she lived, aril they should bother the Government until they gave way, and granted them the power to put down the monstrous drink traffic. They might ask what were the advantages of joining a large national organization. If they joined the organization they would come in contact with;specialists whose whole lives were spent in making a study of the question of in- temperance their one theme. No one, whoever they might be, could come into contact with a specialist without a wonderful gain to themselves. Then the Association was an armytof women banded together for one obj and that object a good one, and they were therefore side by side with thousands of other women, which fact would crush out all feelings of jealousy. They were very proud of their leaders. She did not think that it was possible to have a better one than Lady Henry Somerset. (Applause.) It was a privilege to know of her beautiful consecrated life. She (the speaker) was one who knew a good deal of their president, and to come into contact with her was to be lifted by it. (Ap please.) Alluding to moderate drinkers she said th she never denounced them, but she tried to make them very uncomfortable. (Laughter.) 'I he moderate drinkers might say they did no harm, but did they read it in the Bible that Christ went about doing no harm ? They read that He went about doing good, and every- body who took His name must go about doing good and not lead'a miserable, inactive existence, doing no harm. She, however, combatted the idea that the mcderate drinker did no harm. By their example they did a lot of harm, and it was hard to tell who was a moderate drinker. It seemed to be consi iered that a man who drank just enough to prevent himself from being drunk was a moderate drinker. At any rate, never in the his- tory of this country had moderate drinkers taken so much as at present. She appealed to all moderate drinkers, who might be in the audience to give up the drink for the sake of their children, and she gave several examples in support of her contention that moderate drinkers by their example were the sources of great temptation to many weak persons. She concluded an eloquent address by appealing to her hearers to join in the great movement for the uplifting of England, and to enroll themselves members of the British Women's Temperance Association. (Applause.) During the evening, the proceedings were enlivened by several solos by Miss Bennett, of BewseV. At the close of the meeting a vote of thanks were passed to Mrs. Fairclough for presiding.

WARRINGTON BANKRUPTCY COURT.

FRlDAY.—Before Mr. Registrar Ridgway.

A GRAPPENHALL PUBLICANS BANKRUPTCY.— Charles Eaton, of the Barry Arms Inn, Grappen hall, licensed victualler and farmer. appeared to undergo his public examination in bankruptcy. The gross liabilities were stated to he £lO7 18s. id., and were expected to rank at £B3 75., and the assets were estimated to produce £l2 Bs. 11d. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared on behalf of the debtor. The causes of failure, as alleged by the bankrupt, were competition and bad trade.ln the Official Receiver's observations it was stated that the receiving order was made on bankrupt's own petition. There were two unsatisfied judgments and one action was pending at date of order. The bankrupt stated that he commenced business as a licensed victualler and farmer in 1887, with nearly £2OO capital; and he paid £4O for the_stoek fixtures and goodwill : and that there were four acres of land which he farmed attached to the public-house ; he had not filed the prescribed deficiency account, saying, in lieu that owing to his not having kept any books he could not file a proper deficiency account. Since the Ship Canal was completed the trade at his house bad fallen off; a neighbouring inn had been enlarged, so competition was now greater. His house bad not paid expenses for the last three years.— In reply to Mr. A. B. Potter, the asiistant official re- cemr,.the debtor said the immediate cause of his filing his petition was the pressing for payment by his credi- tors. He. had kept no books as he had newt: done suffi- cient business to need them. He used to sell ten barrels a month but he now only sold four a month.—Tne ex- amination was closed.

BANKRUPTCY OF A BUTCHER.—WiIIiam Worrall, 47 Wash-lane, Latchford, and 515 Knutsford-road, Latch- foid, also underwent his public examination. .The liabi- Mice were £203 12s. id., and the assets were estimated to produce £36 63. ld. Mr. Browne represented the debtor. Causes of failure, as alleged by bankrupt, were bad trade and loss of a colt which cost over £2O. Bank- rupt stated that he commenced business as a butcher, in January, 1894, with £l6 of his own, £l6 given to him by his wife, and £5O lent to him by his father-in-law ; and that he paid £5O for the goodwill and fixtures. He had not filed the prescribed deficieney account, saying, that he could not file a deficiency account, because he had not kept proper books. He lost £2O by the death of a colt. The rest of the deficiency represented loss on trad- ing, mainly through meat going le4d in the summer. He bad been prosecuted and fined for the following offences : —Cruelty to a cow in the sale yard, Warrington, fine £1 13s. ; cruelty to a elleep in the street at Warrington. fine £1 13s. ; cruelty to a horse in the treet at War- rington, fine £l. 135.; keeping a dog without a license, fine 7s. 6d.; having . a diseased cow on his pre- mises' tine £10; all in 1895. He had made a few bets, but his losses during the last two years did not amount to £l. He further stated he had not kept any cash book or creditors' ledger, and that his books did not disclose his fieancial position at any time. In reply to Mr. Potter the debtor said that he lost the colt in May 1895, or thereabouts, He lost most of his custemers when he was fined for a diseased cow, and he also lost money owing to the competition of another bureher. He could net explain the cause of se many prosecutions, ae he •4 did not knew that he was guilty." fle had never het more than a shilling or two in his life, and only went to Man- chester race meeting once a year. He had borrowed money at high rates of interest for the money lenders in the hone of retrieving himself.—The examination was then closed.

Lord Londonderrv, addressing his workmen at the Rainton Collieries, near Durham, on Saturday, said that the total loss on the working. of the mines for last year was over £15,000. To ca'rry on the collieries under present conditions would be a work of a ruinous charac- ter, and in deciding not t,) renew the lease he had not been actuated by caprice,but had seriously and carefully considered the consequences. About 500 men will there- fore shortly be thrown out of work.
