Letter to the Obitor.

NOTICE TO CONTRIBUTORS AND CORRESPON-

DENTS. All letters to the Editor and contributions of news should be written on one side of the paper only.

LIBERALISM. OF 1896 Sir„—paving at the unanimous request of local ,Liberhal Associations contested six almost" forlorn hopes ' for te Liberal be permitted to seek with some de- gree "What t Pai sanrxntioYt'w'4 ijmh eaayL definiteibe rat Programme Until 1880 there could have been no shadow of doubt in this matter—Free Trale, Free Land, Free Cnurch, Free Franchise and Confidence in the Electorate adorned the banner under which we fought. A bold united party held unchagenged the favour of every section of trade and labour throughout the kingdom. Traders, shop- keepers, the salaried and wage-earning classes were loyally Liberal. It was a noble party of which this "nation of shopkeepers " might be justly proud. True to its traditions,the wrongs of Ireland as detailed by Mr. Butt, Q.C., were taken in hand. "Justice to Ireland" became as truly the watchward of the Literal Party as " Free Trade" or the "Abolition of Slavery " in former daye. Had Mr. Butt's moderate demand for " Local Control cf Local Affairs" been adhered to, all would have been well. But the virulence of Irish Land- lord Opposition combined with the ambitious patriotism of Ireland's representatives in Parliament devel- oped these obtainable demands into the readily misinterpreted cry of " In-land a nation." " Separation " became the taunt of the opponents of Ireland's regeneration, add the Liberal party, plunging heart and soul into the Parnellite interpretation of Irish Nationalism, became pledged to a policy which isolated from itself the whilom friends who mistrusted and feared the ultimate issue of the struggle. The defeat of 1886 drew the Libe, al party into still closer association with the destinies of the oppressed Sister Isle, and ulti- mately Ireland, in 1892,—clearly discerning her only true ally—ply ced the Liberal party in cffice,but with so I Blend r a majority, that it became within the power of every knot of " tailors of Tooley-street " to hold in their own hands the reins of party defeat. Much was done for Ireland's agriculture—so much that well might many , an English tenant farmer have wished himself across S. George's Channel. But the Liberal party has paid dearly for its devotion.

The Temperance Section fortified by the suer.cgs on every occasion of Sir Wilfrid Lawson's Local Veto Bill (and following the expansive system of Mr. Butt's Local Government into Mr. Yarnell's Home Rule) no longer demanded only Local Veto but coupled with it robbery from the Licensed Victualler of the Goodwill of his businesg(which he as a lawful tradesman had established) by placing No Compensation' and entire Sunday Clos- ing in the forefront of their battle. We who were Liberal Candidates last year—from the Leader of the House of Commons downwards—too well know with what dire result.

Has not the time arrived, Sir,when the Liberal Party should be able to shake off this grip of " Sectional Ter- rorism "and again become the true and unfettered ex- ponent of universal Freedom and Justice which charac- terised its programrne in 1880. The evils of a Policy of " Nasmyth-Hammering a Tin Tack" have been terribly exemplified. A return to the broader principles of Im- perial and Local Liberalism can alone save the Party from extinction.

The relief of the Imperial Parliament from the encum- brance of local affairs can certainly be secured more readily on the lines of Mr. Butt's Local GQvernment pro- paganda (applicable to every part of the United King- dom) than upon the drastic and somewhat hazardous formula of distinct nationalism. Temperance, too, on the lines of justice and moderation more surely than by spoliation—even if not obtaining at one stroke the full measure of its aspiration. The incorporation by the Liberal Party of Colonial Representation in the Imperial Parliament would alone amply compensate for a little delay in arriving at the consummatinn of each "Sectional Ideal." And assuredly not least readily should be accepted the probability uf such a re-union of the Liberal Party as will enable i to promptly emerge, strengthened and ennobled, from that bottomless pit into which it has apparently already made a deep and most perilous descent.--Your obedient ser- vant, JAMES PIAYSMAN

. (Liberal Candidate for Mile End). Finchley Road, Hampstead. London.

IMAGINARY CONVERSATIONS.

" The jackal can gnaw in safety the tongue of the dead lion. and the field mouse can play its antics in his footsteps on the plains."—Lambert.

Sir,-.-I think you will allow that an imaginary con- versation is as much the privilege of one side as the other. If trifling scepticism can amuse itself in this garb to propagate its follies.reasonable belief may claim to set forth its truths in a similar dress. Plato, Seneca and Peers. Plato (loquitur) : "We have been summoned from our repose, Mr. Peers by a report of certain inquisitions said to have been held betweea ourselves and one Zaminer,' who had properly enough appealed to our utterances upon a subject affecting the highest interests of human kind. We observe though that that subject is not at all represen`ed in this report ; and that the topics which are introduced are so shallow in their treatment, that we must abjure the whole pro- ceedings as a complete fabrication in which we never took part, and which we can only regard as a deep dis- honour done to the reputation of our ancient names. We understand, sir, that you are much of a jester, that you are given to • poking fun,' and adopt Aristophaaic ' methods in serious inquiries. You are aware that the truths of our Socrates were so treated, and he himself done to death at the instance of mere buffoonery. You are here, we understand, to consult our judgment upon the merits of certain Biblia—stand up, sir, and deliver ' the volume you have in hand. Read, Seneca,l the first verse of its opening chapter. I "In the beginning God created the heaven and the Earth." Truely that is a great announcement Seneca: and relieves at once the deep unrest of many an earnest inquirer. The wretched mists which have gathered around the discussions of fools and sophists in every age—are dissipated for ever there." " Verily," replies Seneca, " Still one of their modern writers had, lately need to say ' Fools conceive there is no living God, because they cannot condense His formulas into their small smelling bottles.' " Plato ; " Inotice that the narrative procaeds upon lines admitted by a large class of modern Scientists particularly with reference to , the theory of the nebular hypothesis. Is that not so Mr. Peers?" "Perhaps it is." "Mark noble Plato," interjects Seneca," the introduction of light at the divine mandate." " Yes, it has attracted the admira- tion of the ages and is worthy the voice of a God." "But let me ask Mr. Peers, on whose authority do you speak when you affirm that the sun appears in this sub- lime chapter as created after the earth ?" "The docu ments my Lord Plato" "No, sir, the documents say - no such thing, and are not necessarily so understued Do not some of the ablest scientists understand and in- terpret, that the heavenly bodies, obscured for long ages by the earth's condition of shade, breathe through the obscurity by the concentration of light around the sun, and become by the divine appointment time measurers on the fourth great day or period ?" Peers; "I don't happen to agree with such." "That is you hold a different opinion from theirs ?" Just so." "But does that make right, and all others wrong ?" " Excellert Plato" quoth Seneca "I am reminded of the passage in your Phaede where Socrates is afraid that he had been so far from acting the part of a true philosopher and says " I have behaved myself like a disputant overborne with prejudice as all those ignorants do,who in dispute care not for the perception of the truth ,but mean only to draw their hearers over to their opinions." Plato : " Again, Mr. Peers, are you a lantaniet ?" "Oh yes." "Me there no authorities in that science who would maintain that under conditions of shade, moisture, and heat, a most prolific vegetation might have existed before the directer rays of the sun had pierced the earth's atmosphere ?" "I suppose there are." "Then what is your pretext worth that we have here plants before the creation of the sun,whose creation on that fourth day is not asserted, and upon the testi- mony of competent scholars,need not be so understood ?" Are you the infallible Peers—to whom all authorities must bow'? Equally worthless to us are your geological assumptions. First,as to the nature of the 'six days' or periods which I find also is a Imre question of inter- pretation as both ancient and modern authorities wit- ness. Secondly, upon the testimony of geologists of the highest rank,as Dana and Dawson, to the fact that' in all essential points the first chapter of Genesis and science are in accord.' This disposes, sir, of a number of your gibes as the great lawgiver of the Jews, who, though dead yet speaketb, and after more than 3,000 years are past, is a force in the world's estimation." " Yes," interposes Seneca,. "this jester would strike at the great and honoured dead, the leader of the most remarkable nation that ever rose and fell; but the jackal can gnaw in safety the tongue of the dead lion, and the field mouse can play its antics in his footsteps on the plain." Plato: "An apt quotation. Seneca,from a logical h.thlete ; but where hi Peers ? " "Re is gone, sir, and leaves no trace behind him." "We are spared then his triflings with the New Testament. This modern world Seneca is somewhat of a riddle; we ancients as y9u know lived long in the darkness of relieious night, and had to grope our way as well as we could unaided any certain guide after truth. The result of our highest efforts ended in a cry for divine light. Nineteen hundred rars ago the felt need appeared in the person of Incarnate Truth itself. On a careful examination of the records He came in accordance with a promise made, on some estimations, at least four thousand years previ- ously, and repeated from time to time all alcng the ages, until within a little of His appearing. His advent marked Him out as the very personage described so minutely in a multitude of predictions made many hundreds of years before. His life and teachings, His mighty works, and death and resurrection are recorded upon evidence which the most sifting criticism of the day has not been able to refute. To complete the marvel His mighty in- fluence, as predicted, is daily extending over the whole earth before the eyes of this generation—a miracle in

G A DBU itY'S COCOA. Is a gentle stitnula nt ; it does not Irritate the nerves, but is scothing, refreshing, and invigorating. It is absolutely pure.

INCREASE OF THE MEDICAL OFFICER'S SALARY. Mr. Williams stated that the committee dealing with the Medical Officer's salary had decided to recommend that Dr. Adams' salary be increased from £l2O to £l5O per annum. Since Dr. Adams was appointed in 1883, the work had increased eight fold. His annual repo I t was now as a eixpenny magazine to a penny pamphlet, compared to what it was before Dr. Adams was appointed. Several townships had also been recently added. There were numerous townships in thia Union not accessible by train and the doctor incurred considerable expense in maintaining a hQrse and trap. He was obliged to have two horses owing to the heavy work.—Mr. Brandreth seconded the proposition which was unanimously car- ried.—The Chairman then moved the reappointment of Dr. Adams at a salary of £l5O per annum.—Mr. Williams seconded, and it was carried.—Dr. Adams thanked the Council, and said he would try to do his duty in the future as.in the past.

A meeting of this authority was held in the Boardroom at Dutton Workhouse, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. J. Mclntrye, J.P., occupied the chair,and there were also present :—Admiral Jones- Parry, Messrs. T. Baxter, C.C., J. Brandreth, H. E. Heywood, S. Woodward, J. M. Williams, T. Wright, J. L. Jones. J. W. Baguley, E. Wallington, W. Brad- ford, C. Roylance, T. Pickering, J. Whitlow, E. THE ALLEGED NUISANCE AT FRODSHAII swiox. Mr. C. H. Mason, wrii:ing from the solicitors' offices of the London and North-Western Railway Company, Liverpool, with regard to the alleged nuisance at Frod- sham station, said he had enquired into the mat-er very carefully, and was satisfied that no effort had been spared to meet and remove, as far as possible. the com- plaint of the Council. by dealing with the traffic in ques- tion in a special manner, and at considerable extra trouble and expense to the Joint Railway Companys. He stated that special steps were taken to give early advice to the consignees of the arrival at Frodsham of the waggons containing the bones and shoddy so that they might take prompt delivery, but if they could not do so during the day of arrival, the waggons were taken on by the first convenient train to Helsby, where they remained all night or until such time as the consignees were prepared to take delivery, and then they were brought back to Frodsham, and delivery was at once effected. Strict orders had been issued to the officials not to keep the bones, &c., at Frodsham, when giving off an offensive smell, any longer than was absolutely necessary to deal with them, and these orders bad been carried out. Tne dates in December, when he under- stood the smell was very bad, the general practice was not carried out owing to circumstances which were unavoidable and which could be expltined. The joint companys desired to meet the views of the Council very possible way, and had approached the con—gnees with a view to delivery at Halton, but were unable to obtain their consent. It was considered that the companies being bound to carry the traffic, had dealt with it in a reasonable manner, and that they could not do more than they had done. They had no choice but to fulfil their obligations under the Traffic Act with as little inconven;ence to the public as might be.—Mr. T. Hadfield, of Liverpool,writ- ing on the same subject, said if something was not done to stop the landing of bones, etc., at Frodsham.thereby through the nuisance, ruining his and others' property adjacent to the Frodsham station,he should take prompt action in London to try and compel the Authority to act. —Mr. Wallington said so far as the residents of Sutton were concerned they would strongly object to the ro• moval of the trucks to Halton Station. The smell was awful.—Mr. Knowles said he underatood from the doctor that the smell was not injurious to health.—Mr. Aaron thought the authority had power to put pressure on the railway companies to make sidings to the places where the material was required.—The Clerk said the question was brought before the attention of the Council by Mr. Linaker and Mr. Hadfield.—On the tootion of Mr. Knowles, it was decided that the communication from Mr. Mason should be forwarded to them.

A MRMORIAL FROM FRODSHAM.

The Rev. H. B. Blogg, Frodsham Vicarage, submitted a memorial, remarking that the subject was of great im- portance and something should be done at once. The memorial ran :—" We the undersigned wish to call the attention of the Rural Sanitary Authority to a ventilator opposite Mr. Carpenter's house, Hillsboro', Overton, Frodsham, which we consider to be a public nuisance and a source of danger to the health of the inhabitants of Overton. Signei, Henry B. Blogg, Charles Reynolds, Bessie Gibson, and Julia L. Garratt."—Mr. Carpenter, writing on the same subject, said the present condition of things very seriously affected his property.—Mr. Hut- chins also wrote, stating that a grave nuisance existed by the escape of sewer gas from the grid. It had been stopped up through the danger, and during the last , week it had been re-opened and sewer gas rose with great force among a colony of houses. He was informed that typhoid fever had broken out. He was afraid to bring a family of young children into such a situation. He hoped that the Council would order its closing and the removal of the grid.—lt was resolved that the com- munications should be referred to the Joint Committee. —Mr. Heywood suggested that Dr. Adams should attend the meeting. There was perhaps a little conflict between the two parochial committees on the matter although they had not approached one another. The manhole was blocked up and the consequence was that the sewer gas became so strong that it blew out a ventilating shaft, When the power of the gas was so great there was not the slightest doubt it would be strong enough to find its way into the houses. He would like to have the doctor's opinion on the matter.—Dr. Adams said he would be very glad to attend the meeting. He had got a letter from Dr. Burton who had received several complaints respecting the escape af sewer gas at at the place mentioned.—The Chairman thought the I matter could be dealt with by a joint committee. The subject then dropped. -----

TUB PORT SANITARY AUTHORITY.

The Clerk reported what was done at the enquiry at Manchester-on the previous Tuesday,with respect to the formation of a Port Sanitary Authority for the Ship Canal.—Mr. Brandreth stated that as compared with their rateable value,Runcorn Urban and Runcorn Rural districts would contribute much more than either Salford or Manchester te the estimated expenses of the Port Sanitary Authority.—Mr. Heywood was of the opinion that they shouid raise a protest, and not wait until they felt the hardship. They should do this at once. What they revired was ti ascertain in conjunction with the Runcorn Urban District Council the amount of shipping on the canal showing the ships and their tonnage enter- ing Manchestersand the Runcorn Council's districts,and prepare a comparative statement of the amount that would be required from each of the authorities, repre- sented on the assumed expenditure of £l.BOO a year,and lay it before the Local Government Board before they passed the order, otherwise it would be useless to get an alteration. Be proposed that that be done.-...Mr . Chad- wick seconded. and it was agreed to. TES EHLSBY WATHR SUPPLY.

Mr. Heywood submitted a report of the committee appointed to consider Helsby matters, which showed that the letter from Messrs. Potts, Potts and Gardner was dealt with. It was resolved that the Clerk in reply- ing to it should point out that Mr. Heywood, the chair- man of the committee, did not say anything about the quality of the water or where thesupply would be drawn from ; that the plea on the scale arranged between the Clerk and Mr. Taylor had not yet been furnished, and although a willingness to pay the £5 towards the col of an enquiry by a Local Government Board official had _

been expressed,'no undertaking to pay the amount had yet been given. That therefore the statement made by the Chairman of the committee was substantially correct. That the suggested scale of charges for the supply to cottages in prohibitory, and not such as the Local Gov- ernment Board were likely to approve of, and therefore it was right to describe the scheme as a half scheme. That the reason tbe matter had stood over so long was owing to the company not having complied with the terms imposed, and to the fact that the scale of charges although stipulated by a resolution of the committee was not received until Feb. 4th, 1896. What was required was a plan of the proposed scheme show- ing thereon the proposed pipe line on a scale so ar • ranged between the Clerk and Mr. Taylor, an undertak- ing to pay the £5 in the event of a Local Government Board inquiry being held, and such a scale of charges so far as the supply to cottages is concerned,as the District Council could ask the Local Government Board to sanc- tion, which scale should not exceed a payment of 2d.ner week for cottavea not exceeding a ratable value of £7,or 31 per week if the Local Government Board would sanction It. He moved the adoption of the report.—Mr. Williams seconded, and the report was adopted. THI4 CLHRICS: SALARY,

The Chairman stated that a month ago, a committee was appointed to consider the Clerk's salary. His salary had not been flxei since the amalgamation of the High- way Beard with the Sanitary Authority, and as they were now nearing the end of the year, it was for the Council to decide that day. During the late Mr. Linakees time they had to pay extra for legal work which Mr. Linaker did not undertake to perform. The committee went into the question, and found that the cost to the Sanitary Authority for law charges averaged £BO a year. This work was now done by Mr. Ashton without extra fees. The tetal amount paid including law charges was therefore £235 for the Highway Board and Sanitary Authority, but the work had enormously increased dur- ing the past few years. NDEPENDENT ORDER OF GOOD_ TEMPLERS, TEli-

PERANCE MPSION LODGE, 1321, ACADEMY-ST. The regular weekly meeting of this lodge was held on Wednesday, when the meeting was made specially attractive by the celebration of the Chief Templar's (Brother Hall) 22nd anniversary of his membership in the Order. A plentiful supply of pies and coffee was pravided by Bro. Hall, who delivered an interesting speech, giving the reasons for his identification with Good Templary and some of his reminiscences during that period. A short speech testifying to 25 years in the movement by Bro. Patrick O'Brien was followed by songs by Sisters Nurse and O'Brien, and Bro. Hall, brought a very enjoyable evening to a close. WYCLIFFE P.S.A. TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION.

The usual weekly meeting of this Association was held in the Upper schoolroom on Monday even- ing the president, Rev. John Yonge, in the chair. After the opening address by the Chairman, who urged each individual member of the association to increased effort on behalf of the cause, temper- ance addresses were given by Mr. Patrick O'Brien, and Quarter-Master Sergeant Mantle. Pianoforte duets were given by Mr. Mason and Miss Bradbury and a sympathetic rendering of rennyson's poem, "In the Hospital" brought a very successful even- ing to a close.

CONFIRMATION AT THE PARISH CHURCH. Bishop Royston, of Liverpool, visited the Parish Church on Saturday afternoon, and conducted a confirmation service. THE PRUDENTIAL ASSURANCE

COMPANY, LIMI i ED.

The forty-seventh annual meeting of this Com- pany, which is the largest British Assurance Com- pany, was held on March 5, and the report published shows that the vast business is extending more and more each year, a fact upon which they are to be congratulated. It is a tribute to the system upon which the Company is worked that the accounts are amongst the first to be published in the new year. A few figures will be sufficient to show the enormous business done during twelve months, and we therefore give a few extracts from the report. IQ the ordinary branch which is only a recent creation, the number of policies issued during the year was 61,450, which assured the sum of £6,285,280. The premium revenne was £2,304,013, by an increase of £226,057, on the previous year, equal to the total premiums of some of the most progressive offices. In the industrial branch, the premiums received in 1893 amounted to £4,352,625, and altheugh the increase was not equal to that of 1894, the progressive character of the business continued to be maintained, being £108.401 ahead of the precedicg year, The result of last year's operations was a surplus of £2,702,085 increasing the Company's assets to the tremendous total of £23,915,890. It is thus very clear that the Prudential continues Co prosper, and that under its splendid management it will continue to pro:- per. One satisfactory feature in the industrial branch is that if a policy holder fails to keep up his policy be does not thereby make a loss, because a free policy is granted in lieu thereof. The number of policies so dealt with in 1895 was 59,352, and the number in force at the close of the year was 448,816. The industrial public may, therefore, have full confidence in the Pru- dential, and they never need hesitate to take out a policy. One item in the report states that the Company are making an attempt to deal with the old age pension question. One great difficulty of old age pensions is that the stability of the institution which provides them must be beyond all doubt, and that can certainly be said of the Prudential, which has funds amounting to nearly £24,000,000 sterling. A scheme has been worked out in September, and the number of policies of tiiis kind was by the close of the year, 169,791,producing an annual premium income of £62,974. The scheme has been so arranged that on the maturity of the endowment the assured may either take a lump som of money or take out an annuity. The policies are all on easy terms, and a most profitable investment can thus be made by the public. It has sometimes been alleged against In- dustrial Assurance Companies that the profits are made from the lapses, but this cannot be said of Prudential. Every effort is made to keep the policies in force,and the success act ieved in this direction is evident from the fact that the average age of the industrial policies, not- withstanding the large new business of recent years is nearly 71 years, a fact which speaks for itself. It is very evident from the facts and figures given above, that a more substantial and successful establishment does not exist than the Prudential Assurance Company, and it is one of the safest to invest in.

FINANCE. -THE ESTIMATES.

Mr. Brandreth moved the adoption of the report of the Finance Committee, recommending the payment of ac- mints amounting to £990 53. The balance in hand was £2,851 103. 10(1. 'The half yearly report and estimate showed that the probable expenditure during the ensu- ing half-year ending Michaelmas, 1896, was :—General expenses, public health, £757 ; highway expenses, £1,892 ; and balance and contingency fund £2,061, total £4,710. After deducting the probable balance, the amount required was £2.714. The estimate for high- way expenses was based upon the surveyor's estimates for the year,according to which the probable expenditure for highway purposes would be 6d. in the £ on the rate- able value of the district. It was considered that if a call of Id. in the £ was made this half year for sar-itary expenses,and 21(1. for highway purposes, it would be sufficient for the half-year ending Michaelmas, and a further call might be made afterwards of 311. in the £ for highway purposes. The rateable value of the dis- trict as per valuation listd in force was £181,619, a de- ' crease of £122 compared with the previous year. A rate of 41. in the £ would produce £2,649, and it was re- commended that a call for the sum of £2,714 be made upon the overseers,a decrease of id. in the £ on the cor- responding period last year.—Mr. Heywood seconded the adoption of the report of the committee which was approved of.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST BAZAAR,

EA RLESTOWN.

At the openine ceremony on Friday, there were present the Revs. T. R. Matthews, E. R. Davies, F. E. Miller and J. Allatt, Dr. Patterson, Mr. E. Dickens and others. Mr. Thomas Ormand presided, and after the reading of a passage of scripture by the Rev. E. R. Davies,and prayer by the Rev. F. E. Miller, the Chairman said he had one important apology to tender to them that afternoon and that was on behalf of the weather. They regretted it very much in the first place because it had kept many persons away who would have been glad to come and help them and increase the audience to welcome Mr. Borron who had come to assist them. He noticed several persons there who were present the previous day and he hoped they would pardon him if he traversed the ground referred to by Mr. Davies. They would readily understand why they were putting forth every effort to relieve themselves e the debt, and he was certain Mr. Borron would appeal not only to their hearts but to their pockets. He we, sure that they would tender to him a most hearty welcome. (Applause.)—Mr. C. B. F. Borron said bazaars seemed to him to be something like some of the community of the neighbourhood, they were always with them. He believed there was a superabundance of energy on the part of the ladies in furthering this good work. It struck him this must be a phase of the new woman, but be that as it may, he was sure the object they had assembled that day for was one which he trusted would have the assistance not only of the ladies of the district.but of the whole community. (Applause.) The object of the bazaar, as stated, was for the liquida- tion of a debt which had been a burden upon the Com- mittee, and which they were using every effort to relieve themselves of, and in that effort they had been supported in by a large contributor. He therefore appealed to all th,re to place the committee in as position a so to secure the furtherance of the end in view. They could, gener- ally speaking, work in unison for an object which was for the welfare of every party. He then formerly declared the bazaar open. The Rev. E. R. Davies moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Borron. He was more pleased to move that vote be- cause Mr. Borron happened to belong to another church than their own. He did not even belong to the Metho- dist connection, in its widest form. This was as it should be, they should live together in unity,and Earles- town was learning to do that,as well as any other town. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. E. Davies seconded, and the vote was heartily accorded. The sale was then proceeded with and carried on until late in the evening. Owing to the inclement weather, the attendance was not so large as it otherwise would have been, the takings for the day amounting to a little over £4O. The opening ceremony was performed on Saturday by Mr. P.Thompson, M.B. and Mr. E. J. George presiding. There was a fair attendance during the afternoon,which was largely augmented in the evening. On Friday even- ing the Earlestown Orchestral Society gave an instru- mental concert in the lecture hall, and later on two con- certs were given, the first arranged by Mrs. Latimer, A.R.C.0., and the second by Mr. E. Leyland. On Satur- day afternoon there was a children's concert, the pro- gramme being arranged by Mrs. Rawlings and Mr. A. Barton. In the evening a concert arranged by Mr. W. arton was given, and later on two exhibitions of the living waxworks, each of which was exceptionally well attended. As the bazaar drew to a close the stalls wore an vi teehrmy t appearance, this being especially noticeable empty ygrocery stall which was practically cleared out. Everybody body worked hard to ensure suceess, and the re- suit will show that their efforts put forth have not been futile. The bulk of the work of course fell upon the hon. sec., Mr. T. Ormand, and the hon. assis. sec., Mr. W. Barton. Mr. F. R. Bagnall officiated as treasurer, and the Rev. E. R. Davies was president af the Commit- tee which was a large one, including many members of the congregation. Close on midnight on Saturday, when the sale was over, cheers were given for the workers, and for all who had contributed to the success of the bazaar, by their presence and help. RUNCORN AND WIDNES DISTRICT TEACHERS' ASSOCIATION.

WIDNES SCHOOL BOARD AND THEIR SCHOOLS. A meeting of the above-mentioLed Association which is the local branch of the National Unpn of Teachers, was held in the Conservative Club, Widnes, on Satur- day evening. There was an exceptionally: large atten- dance of Whines and Runcorn teachers, including the president (Mr. Moulton), vice-president (Mr. Snoddy), and secretary (Mr. B. Crosby). The Secretary reported the result ot the :correspondenee he had had with the Widnes School Board with refer- ence to the hire of a classroom at the West Bank Schools for teachers' meetings. The Clerk of the Board had written to say that the School Management Committee weuld grant the use of a small room for the teachers at 83. 61. per night, and a large room for 17s. This was looked upon as excessive, and at the last mert- ing, the secretary was instructed to write Mr. F. Niel, chairman of the School Manage- ment Committee, asking him to receive a de- putation from the association. That gentleman had replied, declining to accede to the request. A pro- longed conversation took place with reference to the pro- posed charges which were looked upon as prohibitive, and it was ultimately decided that the secretary should write to Mr. H. Wade Deacon, the chairman of the Board, explaining to him the fact that nine-tenths of the work of the teachers' union was purely educational and benevolent, and asking that the Board would further consider the claim of the teachers to the hire of the room at the lower educational rate, according to the Board's scale of charges. The voting for the vice-presidency and executive of the National Union of Teachers was then proceeded with. When the papers were collected it was found that of the 47 members of the association who were entitled to vote, 37 had exercised their franchise, this being higher than on any previous occasion. The result of the election will be announced at the forthcoming conference of teachers to be held at Brighton during Easter week. The represen- tatives of the local association to this conference are Mr. Cookson (Weston Point), Mr Crosby and Mr. Moulton (Runcorn), and Mr. Snoddy (Widnes). Two new mem- bers were nominated and accepted by the Association.

OF MANCHESTER'S PALACES OF INDUSTRY.

INTERESTING DETAILS.

THB Great City of Manchester is undoubtedly the

largest centre of commerce in the world. It is. next to Lon- don, the most important city in the British empire; its commercial enterprise is famous throughout civilisation, and has even penetrated to the daraest recesses of barbaric humanity. Manchester is the centre of a population within 12 miles of her Town Hall as great as London, the whole of which is engatted in the cause of labour. Day by day the powerful and mighty engines of steam power are, under the dual control of the mech nic and operative, busily eagaged in contributing to the successful trading ot this marvellously active and industrious community. lit my visit to this won- derful city I chanced to discover amongst the many gigantic and colossal hives of industry a well-known famous furnish: lug establishment,

`` THE PANTHEON."

Suffice it to say that "The Pantheon" is indisputably the very largest Furnishing Establishment in the North of Eng- land exclusively engaged in Furnishing It is not a Drapery Establishment, but is a solid, compact, massive warehouse containing over a million square feet of Showrooms packed from the basement to the root by the Finest Stock of Furni- ture open for inspection outside the city of London. The des- tinies of this great establishment, I find, are presided over ty Mr. W. H. Worsley, the sole and absolute proprietor, to whose untiring energy and unflagging enterprise "The Pantheon "owes its high position. WIDNES COUNTY COURT.

BETTING ON A RUNCORN FOOTBALL me:rca.

On Friday, at the Widnes C- unty Court, before hitt Honour Judge Shand, Charles H. Watson sued Joseph' Bell for £4. The plaintiff was not legally represented, but Mr. J. 0. Swift, of St. Helens, appeared for the de- fendant.—The plaintiff stated that he entered into a betting transaction with the defendant, which resulted hi his winning £4. The bet, was that defendant laid L 4 to £2 that Runcorn would beat Oldham in their match at Runcorn on January 4.—The Judge said he was afraid the plaintiff had put himself out of court, as a bet could not be recovered by law.—The plaintiff said a friend of his stood in, he (plaintiff) laying 15s. to £l, and be had to pay this £1 155. He, however, could not obtain the money due to him.—His Honour: You have been what in ordinary terms is called "welched ? "—Plain- tiff :—No, I have too much respect for the defendant tO say that.—By Mr. Swift: The transaction took place some days before the match was played, and he laid the money with his friend before the Saturday. Plaintiff' answered the questions in a loud tone of voice, which called forth from the Judge the remark, "Keep quiet, we are not in the ring now."—Further cross-examined, the plaintiff admitted that the defendant sent word d" daring the bet off. Plaintiff declined to say where bet was made. He would write the name of the house on a bit of paper.—Mr. Swift did not press for the naMeir but plaintiff admitted that it was made in a publichouSe. Plaintiff said he considered the money due to him ag commission.—Henry Barber of the Widnes Football CIO' was called by the plaintiff, and said he was present when Ithe bet was made. He considered it a perfectly fair 130'1 as Runcorn had not been defeated on their own grounQ' lup to then.—Mr. Swift said he submitted that by lair the plaintiff was not entitled to recover, and the quo" tion of the friend did not enter into the case. The da: fendant did not remember the circumstances of the bel and when he found a paper in his pocket he at °meetly word to. the pia ntiff declaring the bet " off."---A% Honour, in giving judgment said the question of age:n did not enter into that case! It was a bet made heti" the two parties, and he must noosuit the plaintiff.

ORDINARY BRANCH.—The number of Policies issued during the year was 61,450, assuring the sum of £6,285,260, 'ind producing a New Annual Premium Income of t 342.478. •jtie Premiums received during the year were £2,304,013, an increase of £226.057 over the year 1894. Nhe Claims of the year am ,unted to £572,289. The number h." Deaths was 4,479, and 218 Endowment Assurances `ilitured. The number of Policies in force at the end of the year was 1„_4.137. ,kkeiDUSTRIAL BRANCH —The Premiums received during year were £4,352,625. being an increase of £108.401. che Claims of the year amounted to £4797,688. The num- ber of Deaths was 196,507, and 1,418 Endowment Assurances .Latured. , The number of Free Policies granted during the year to ' those policyholders of five years' standing, wha desired to ' discontinue their payments, was 59,352, Ih number in force being 448,816. The number of Free Policies which became Claims during the year was 8.956. The Directors have made more than one attempt to deal 'With the difficult que tion of old age pensions for the Indus- trial classes, and they are happy to inform the Shareholders that the special tables combining assurance with a provision Of old age, which they issued in September last, have met With considerable success At the end of the year the num- ber of Policirs In force under these tables as the result of three months' working was 169,731, producing an Annual Premium Income of £62.974. TO THE ELECTORS OF THE STOCK TON HEATH WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN.— My term of office as one of your representatives on the Parish Council having expired, I hereby solicit the renewal of your patronage. In again offering my services I need scarcely remind the Ratepsyers that if I am elected it will be my endeavour to serve them in a thoroughly conscientious manner and to watch their interests in the Council in the future as in the past. I will support those schemes which will he to the advantage of the Parish, at the same time having in view at all times the desirability of keeping the rates as low as pos- sible consistent with the requirements of this fast growing Parish. I therefore respectfully appeal to the Ratepayers for their support on the day of the Poll. am, Ladies and Gentlemen, Your obedient Servant. WILLIAM COPPOCK. March 18th, 1896.

DISTRICT COUNCIL ELECTION. TO THE ELECTORS OF THE VIADUCT WARD. LADIES AND GENTLEMEN,— Having been requested to stand once more as a Candidate for the Viaduct ward at the coming election, I williogly accede to the gequest, and, if elected, will give my very earnest attention to the interests of the district, as I have eudeavoured to do during my past term of office. I had hoped that the improvements I promised to advo- cate in my last address, would have been begun, if not com- pleted, by this time. By whose fault these improvements have been neglected, I leave the public to judge. In order to serve the interests of the district I have done my work with- out fear or favour, and ir this course of action is branded "obstruction "I am perfectly willing to be known and to proclaim myself an" obstructionist." If elected, I shall not in the future—as I have not in the past—be deterred by either appfobrious epitkets or votes of censure from doing what I consider my duty to the rate_ payers, whose interests I have, against many odds, tried con- scientiously to serve during the past year. I therefore place my services in your hands, and confi- dently await your decision on the day of election. I am, ladies and gentlemen, , Your obedient servant, I'd-McMATION. Market-street, Earlestown, March 16th, 1896. •

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENE- RAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY OF CHESTER will be held at the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFCRD, in and for the said County, on TUESDAY, the 7th day of April next. at half-past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hun- dreds of Bucklow, Macclesfield, and Northwich, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Hundreds, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford District must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on TUESDAY, the 7th day of April next, at half•past eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE Court will be adjourned to the Castle of CHESTER. on THURSDAY the 9th day of April next, at eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Hundreds of Broxton, Edelisbury, Nantwich, and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from thb last-mentioned Hun- dreds will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the epening of the Court, on THURSDAY, the 9th day of April next, at tleven o'clock in the forenoon. REGINALD POTTS, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester, Cheater, 16th March, 1895.

A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN The information containecl In this book ought to be known by every married woman, and it will not hary. the unmarried to real The book is conveniently divided into 12 chapters. The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty,or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doetor's standpoint_,- points out the best age for marriage. and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful in- formation that one can ordinarily get only from an in- telligent doctor. The third eh pter treats of the marriage of blood relations; rad condemns such marriages ass rule. Chapter four treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fifth chapter tells how a woman should live during the pregnant state. The sixth cha ,ter treats ot mishaps and how to avoid them. The 'sevenrh chapter treats of maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due tolongings on Ihe part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy if certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The ninth chapter treats of tho proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is U p and about again. The eleventh ohapter treats of sterility ; elves the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the "change," most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and. no book is written which goes so thoroughly into matters relating to married women Some may think too much Is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is Power and the means of attaining !looniness. The book can he bed from Dr. T• R. AILINSON, 25 Box. 4, Spanish-place Manchester-square, London, W., in return for a Postal erder for is. ld.

The Rugby football match at Glasgow on Saturday between England and Scotland ended in a victory for the Scotclimen by a goal and two tries to nil. At Dub- lin, Ireland defeated Wales by a goal and a try to a dropped goal, and thus won the international champion- ship. Wills Harper, a Liverpool solicitor, was on Saturday sentenced at the Liverpool Assizes to three years' penal servitude for forging deeds inr the transfer of property by means of which he fraudulently obtained between £2,000 and £2,500. Mr. Jclin Burns, M.P., addressing his constituents on Sunday evening, criticised the Workmen's Dwelling Bill as bristling with absurdities, and as likely to be a fruit- ful source of jobbery for the well-to-do ; referred to the proposed pension to the Duke of Camt,tidgFt as a " gross public scandal ;" and deprecated the system of secret diplomacy by which troDps were sent up to Dongol a from Egypt.

HALTON. JUVENILE MISSIONARY MEETING.—This meeting waS held in the Wesleyan Sunday School on Sunday after- noon, under the presidency of Mr. William istbury. Missionary addresses were delivered by Messrs. A. Rowe, C. H. Smith, T. Leathwooci, W. Looker, F. Hankey,and J. Clarke. Several musical items were agreeably inter- spersed. A quartette entitled " Sweet Galilee," was wel rendered by Miss M. Newbury and Miss C. Looker, M C. P. Bradbury and Miss P. Rowlinson. The Misses . and A Looker contributed a duet " Hiding in Thee," and Miss Pheebe Barrow a solo. Mr. Sutton was the ac-' companist. The senior superintendent of the school (Mr. H. Rowe), distributed a number of prizes to the missionary collectors. WESLEY GUILD.—" A LADIES' EVENING."—The Rev.o De Putron Gliddon presided at a meeting held in connec tion with this guild on MJnday evening, when the pro- gramme was provided by the lady members of the guild. There was a gcod attendance, and the proceed- ings were of a most enjoyable character. The pro- gramme included recitations by Miss A. Hankey, Mrs. Aitken, Miss Baguley, and Miss Boys. Miss Rowe cor- tributed a reading. The vocal items were duets by ti Misses C. and A. Looker, Miss M.Newbury, and Miss A. Sanders, and Mrs. Moores and Miss Howard, also a sole by Mrs. Faulkner. The duties of accompanists were fulfilled by Mrs. Gliddon and Miss Boys.
